    - (i) 5 members personally present, if the number of members on the date of the meeting is not more than 1,000;
    - (ii) 15 members personally present, if the number is more than 1,000 but up to 5,000;
    - (iii) 30 members personally present, if the number exceeds 5,000;
  - (b) for a private company, 2 members personally present.
(2) If the quorum is not present within half an hour of the appointed time:
  - (a) the meeting stands adjourned to the same day in the next week at the same time and place, or to such other date, time and place as the Board determines; or
  - (b) a meeting called by requisitionists under section 100 stands cancelled.
  For an adjourned meeting where the day, time or place is changed, the company must give at least 3 days' notice to members, either individually or by advertisement in one English and one vernacular newspaper circulating where the registered office is situated.
(3) If at the adjourned meeting the quorum is again not present within half an hour of the appointed time, the members present constitute the quorum.

### Section 104. Chairman of meetings
(1) Unless the articles provide otherwise, the members personally present elect one of themselves as chairman by show of hands.
(2) If a poll is demanded on the election of the chairman, it is taken under the Act, and the chairman elected by show of hands continues until another person is elected chairman as a result of the poll, who then chairs the rest of the meeting.

### Section 105. Proxies
(1) Any member entitled to attend and vote at a meeting may appoint another person as proxy to attend and vote on his behalf, subject to these limits:
  - a proxy has no right to speak at the meeting;
  - a proxy may vote only on a poll;
  - unless the articles provide otherwise, this sub-section does not apply to a company without share capital;
  - the Central Government may prescribe classes of companies whose members may not appoint proxies;
  - a person may act as proxy for not more than 50 members and for members holding in aggregate not more than 10% of the total voting share capital, as prescribed.
(2) Every notice calling a meeting of a company with share capital (or other company whose members may appoint proxies) must state with reasonable prominence that a member may appoint a proxy and that the proxy need not be a member.
(3) If default is made in complying with (2), every officer in default is liable to a penalty of ₹5,000 (2019 amendment).
(4) Any provision in the articles requiring the proxy instrument or other document to be lodged more than 48 hours before a meeting is ineffective to that extent; a 48-hour limit applies instead.
(5) If invitations to appoint a named person (or one of named persons) as proxy are issued at the company's expense to some members only, every officer who knowingly issues or authorises them is punishable with fine up to ₹1 lakh [verify whether converted to penalty]. This does not apply where a member asks in writing for the proxy form or list of willing proxies, if the form or list is available to every member on written request.
(6) The proxy instrument must be in writing and signed by the appointer or his authorised attorney, or, for a body corporate, be under its seal or signed by an authorised officer or attorney, and be in the prescribed form.
(7) A proxy instrument in the prescribed form is not invalid merely because it does not follow the articles' form. [verify]
(8) Every member entitled to vote may inspect the proxies lodged, during the period from 24 hours before the meeting until its conclusion, during business hours, on giving at least 3 days' written notice.

### Section 106. Restriction on voting rights
(1) The articles may provide that a member may not vote on shares registered in his name on which calls or other sums remain unpaid, or on which the company has exercised a right of lien.
(2) The company may not prohibit a member from voting on any other ground.
(3) On a poll, a member with more than one vote need not use all his votes or cast them all the same way.

### Section 107. Voting by show of hands
(1) At a general meeting, a resolution is decided on a show of hands unless a poll is demanded under section 109 or voting is carried out electronically under section 108.
(2) The chairman's declaration that a resolution has been carried (unanimously or by a particular majority) or lost on a show of hands, and its entry in the minute book, is conclusive evidence of the fact without proof of the number or proportion of votes cast.

### Section 108. Voting through electronic means
The Central Government may prescribe the class(es) of companies and the manner in which a member may exercise his right to vote by electronic means. (Under the Rules: listed companies and companies with not less than 1,000 members [verify].)

### Section 109. Demand for poll
(1) Before or on declaration of the result of a show of hands, the chairman may order a poll on his own motion, and must order one on demand made:
  - (a) in a company with share capital, by members present in person or by proxy holding at least one-tenth of total voting power, or holding shares on which at least ₹5 lakh (or higher prescribed amount) has been paid up; and
  - (b) in any other company, by members present in person or by proxy having at least one-tenth of total voting power.
(2) The persons who demanded the poll may withdraw the demand at any time.
(3) A poll on the election of the chairman or on adjournment is taken forthwith; a poll on any other question is taken at such time, not later than 48 hours from the demand, as the chairman directs.
(4) The chairman must appoint such number of persons as he considers necessary as scrutinisers to scrutinise the poll and report to him.
(5) The chairman may remove a scrutiniser and fill any vacancy in that office.
(6) The poll is taken in the prescribed manner, subject to the Act.
(7) The result of the poll is deemed to be the decision of the meeting on the resolution concerned.

### Section 110. Postal ballot
(1) Notwithstanding anything in the Act, a company:
  - (a) must transact by postal ballot such items of business as the Central Government notifies as to be transacted only by postal ballot; and
  - (b) may transact by postal ballot any item of business other than ordinary business and any business on which directors or auditors have a right to be heard,
  in the prescribed manner. An item that must be passed by postal ballot may instead be transacted at a general meeting by a company that is required to provide electronic voting under section 108, in the manner provided there (2017 amendment).
(2) A resolution assented to by the requisite majority through postal ballot is deemed duly passed at a general meeting convened for the purpose.

### Section 111. Circulation of members' resolution
(1) On written requisition by the number of members required under section 100, a company must:
  - (a) give its members notice of any resolution that may properly be moved and is intended to be moved at a meeting; and
  - (b) circulate to members any statement relating to a proposed resolution or business to be dealt with at that meeting.
(2) The company need not act under (1) unless a copy of the requisition signed by the requisitionists is deposited at the registered office:
  - (a) at least 6 weeks before the meeting, for a requisition requiring notice of a resolution; and
  - (b) at least 2 weeks before the meeting, for any other requisition,
  and a sum reasonably sufficient to meet the company's expenses is deposited or tendered. If an AGM is called for a date 6 weeks or less after the copy is deposited, the copy is deemed properly deposited.
(3) The company need not circulate a statement if the Central Government, on application by the company or an aggrieved person, is satisfied that the rights are being abused to secure needless publicity for defamatory matter.
(4) On such an application, the Central Government may order the company's costs to be paid wholly or partly by the requisitionists, even if they are not parties.
(5) If default is made in complying with this section, the company and every officer in default are liable to a penalty of ₹25,000 (2019 amendment).

### Section 112. Representation of President and Governors in meetings
(1) The President of India or the Governor of a State, if a member of a company, may appoint any person to act as representative at any meeting of the company or of any class of members.
(2) The person so appointed is deemed a member and may exercise the same rights and powers (including voting by proxy and postal ballot) as the President or Governor could as a member.

### Section 113. Representation of corporations at meeting of companies and of creditors
(1) A body corporate (whether or not a company under this Act) may, by resolution of its Board or other governing body, authorise a person:
  - (a) if it is a member of a company, to act as its representative at any meeting of the company or of any class of members; or
  - (b) if it is a creditor (including a debenture-holder) of a company, to act as its representative at any meeting of creditors held under the Act or under any instrument.
(2) The authorised person may exercise on behalf of the body corporate the same rights and powers (including voting by proxy and postal ballot) as it could if it were an individual member, creditor or debenture-holder.

### Section 114. Ordinary and special resolutions
(1) A resolution is an ordinary resolution if the required notice has been duly given and the votes cast in its favour (including any casting vote of the chairman), by show of hands, electronically or on a poll, in person, by proxy or by postal ballot, exceed the votes cast against it by members entitled and voting.
(2) A resolution is a special resolution when:
  - (a) the intention to propose it as a special resolution is specifically stated in the notice of the general meeting or other intimation;
  - (b) the required notice has been duly given; and
  - (c) the votes cast in favour (by the same modes) are at least three times the votes cast against it by members entitled and voting.

### Section 115. Resolutions requiring special notice
Where the Act or the articles require special notice of a resolution, notice of the intention to move it must be given to the company by members holding at least 1% of total voting power, or holding shares on which at least ₹5 lakh has been paid up, as prescribed; and the company must give its members notice of the resolution in the prescribed manner. (Under the Rules, special notice is given not earlier than 3 months but at least 14 days before the meeting, and the company notifies members at least 7 days before the meeting [verify].)

### Section 116. Resolutions passed at adjourned meeting
A resolution passed at an adjourned meeting of the company, of holders of any class of shares, or of the Board, is treated for all purposes as having been passed on the date it was actually passed, and not on any earlier date.

### Section 117. Resolutions and agreements to be filed
(1) A copy of every resolution or agreement covered by (3), together with the explanatory statement under section 102 (if any), must be filed with the Registrar within 30 days of its passing or making, in the prescribed manner and with prescribed fees, within the time specified in section 403.
(2) If a company fails to file within the period in (1), the company is liable to a penalty of ₹10,000 plus ₹100 per day for continuing failure, up to ₹2 lakh; and every officer in default, including any liquidator, is liable to a penalty of ₹10,000 plus ₹100 per day, up to ₹50,000 (2020 amendment).
(3) This section applies to:
  - (a) special resolutions;
  - (b) resolutions agreed to by all members that would otherwise have needed to be passed as special resolutions;
  - (c) Board resolutions or company agreements relating to appointment, re-appointment, renewal of appointment, or variation of terms of appointment, of a managing director;
  - (d) resolutions or agreements agreed by any class of members, which would not have been effective unless passed by a specified majority or in a specified manner, and all resolutions or agreements that bind all members of a class though not agreed to by all of them;
  - (e) resolutions passed by the company consenting to the Board's exercise of powers under section 180(1)(a) and (c);
  - (f) resolutions requiring a company to be wound up voluntarily under section 304 [as it stood; voluntary winding up now largely under the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 2016] [verify];
  - (g) resolutions passed under section 179(3). Provisos: no person may inspect or obtain copies of resolutions filed under this clause under section 399 (2015 amendment); this clause does not apply to resolutions of a banking company, NBFC or housing finance company in the ordinary course of business relating to grant of loans or provision of guarantee or security for loans (2017 amendment); and private companies are exempt from this clause [verify source — exemption notification/2017 amendment];
  - (h) any other resolution or agreement that may be prescribed and placed in the public domain.

### Section 118. Minutes of proceedings of general meeting, meeting of Board of Directors and other meeting and resolutions passed by postal ballot
(1) Every company must ensure that minutes of proceedings of every general meeting, of every meeting of any class of shareholders or creditors, of every resolution passed by postal ballot, and of every meeting of its Board or Board committee, are prepared and signed in the prescribed manner and kept within 30 days of the conclusion of each meeting (or of the postal ballot), in books kept for that purpose with consecutively numbered pages.
(2) The minutes must contain a fair and correct summary of the proceedings.
(3) All appointments made at any of the meetings must be included in the minutes.
(4) Minutes of Board and committee meetings must also contain:
  - (a) the names of directors present; and
  - (b) for each resolution, the names of directors, if any, who dissented from or did not concur with it.
(5) Nothing may be included in the minutes that, in the chairman's reasonable opinion:
  - (a) is or could reasonably be regarded as defamatory of any person;
  - (b) is irrelevant or immaterial to the proceedings; or
  - (c) is detrimental to the interests of the company.
(6) The chairman has absolute discretion to include or exclude any matter on the grounds in (5).
(7) Minutes kept in accordance with this section are evidence of the proceedings recorded.
(8) Where minutes have been kept in accordance with this section, then, until the contrary is proved, the meeting is deemed duly called and held, all proceedings at it deemed duly taken, and all appointments of directors, KMP, auditors or company secretaries in practice made at it deemed valid.
(9) No document purporting to report the proceedings of a general meeting may be circulated or advertised at the company's expense unless it includes the matters required to be contained in the minutes.
(10) Every company must observe the secretarial standards on general and Board meetings specified by the Institute of Company Secretaries of India under section 3 of the Company Secretaries Act, 1980 and approved by the Central Government.
(11) If default is made in complying with this section, the company is liable to a penalty of ₹25,000 and every officer in default to a penalty of ₹5,000 (2019 amendment).
(12) A person found guilty of tampering with the minutes of any meeting is punishable with imprisonment up to 2 years and fine of not less than ₹25,000, extending to ₹1 lakh.

### Section 119. Inspection of minute-books of general meeting
(1) Books containing minutes of general meetings and postal ballot resolutions must be kept at the registered office and be open during business hours to inspection by any member without charge, subject to reasonable restrictions under the articles or by general meeting.
(2) Any member is entitled to be furnished with a copy of any such minutes within 7 working days of his request, on payment of the prescribed fees.
(3) If inspection is refused or a copy is not furnished within the specified time, the company is liable to a penalty of ₹25,000 and every officer in default to a penalty of ₹5,000, for each refusal or default (2019 amendment).
(4) In case of such refusal or default, the Tribunal may by order direct immediate inspection of the minute-books or direct that the copy be sent forthwith.

### Section 120. Maintenance and inspection of documents in electronic form
Without prejudice to other provisions of the Act, any document, record, register, minutes, etc.:
  - (a) required to be kept by a company; or
  - (b) allowed to be inspected or copies given to any person,
may be kept, inspected or copied in electronic form, in the prescribed form and manner.

### Section 121. Report on annual general meeting
(1) Every listed public company must prepare, in the prescribed manner, a report on each AGM, including a confirmation that the meeting was convened, held and conducted in accordance with the Act and rules.
(2) A copy of the report must be filed with the Registrar within 30 days of the conclusion of the AGM, with prescribed fees, within the time specified in section 403.
(3) If the company fails to file the report before the expiry of the specified period, the company is liable to a penalty of ₹1 lakh plus ₹500 per day for continuing failure, up to ₹5 lakh; and every officer in default to a penalty of ₹25,000 plus ₹500 per day, up to ₹1 lakh (2020 amendment).

### Section 122. Applicability of this Chapter to One Person Company
(1) Section 98 and sections 100 to 111 (both inclusive) do not apply to a One Person Company.
(2) Where, for an OPC, any business must be transacted at an AGM or other general meeting by ordinary or special resolution, it is sufficient if the resolution is communicated by the member to the company and entered in the minute-book under section 118, signed and dated by the member.
(3) Where an OPC has only one director on its Board, any business required to be transacted at a Board meeting is sufficiently transacted if the resolution is entered in the minute-book under section 118 and signed and dated by that director; that date is deemed the date of the Board meeting for all purposes of the Act.
(4) For the purposes of (2), the date of signing by the member is deemed the date of the meeting for all purposes of the Act.

## CHAPTER VIII — DECLARATION AND PAYMENT OF DIVIDEND

### Section 123. Declaration of dividend
(1) A company may declare or pay dividend for a financial year only:
  - (a) out of its profits for that year, arrived at after providing for depreciation under (2), or out of undistributed profits of any previous year(s) arrived at after providing for depreciation, or out of both; or
  - (b) out of money provided by the Central or a State Government for payment of dividend under a guarantee given by that Government.
  Provisos and explanation:
  - before declaring dividend, the company may transfer to its reserves such percentage of its profits for that year as it considers appropriate;
  - where, owing to inadequacy or absence of profits, the company proposes to declare dividend out of accumulated profits of previous years transferred to free reserves, it must do so in accordance with the prescribed rules (Rules: rate not exceeding the average of the 3 preceding years; amount drawn not exceeding one-tenth of paid-up capital plus free reserves; amount first applied to set off losses of the year; balance reserves not to fall below 15% of paid-up share capital) [verify];
  - no dividend may be declared or paid out of reserves other than free reserves;
  - no dividend may be declared unless carried-over previous losses and depreciation not provided in previous years are set off against profit for the current year (2015 amendment);
  - Explanation (2017 amendment): in computing profits, unrealised gains, notional gains, revaluation of assets, and changes in carrying amount of assets or liabilities on fair-value measurement are excluded.
(2) Depreciation is to be provided in accordance with Schedule II.
(3) The Board may declare interim dividend during any financial year, or at any time between the close of the financial year and the AGM, out of the surplus in the profit and loss account, or out of profits of the financial year for which it is declared, or out of profits generated in the quarter preceding the declaration (2017 amendment). If the company has incurred a loss during the current financial year up to the end of the quarter immediately preceding the declaration, the interim dividend may not exceed the average rate declared during the immediately preceding 3 financial years.
(4) The dividend amount, including interim dividend, must be deposited in a separate account in a scheduled bank within 5 days of declaration.
(5) Dividend is payable only to the registered shareholder, to his order, or to his banker, and only in cash. This does not prohibit capitalisation of profits or reserves to issue fully paid bonus shares or to pay up amounts unpaid on members' shares. Explanation: "cash" includes payment by cheque, dividend warrant or electronic mode.
(6) A company that fails to comply with sections 73 and 74 (deposits) may not declare dividend on its equity shares while the failure continues.

### Section 124. Unpaid Dividend Account
(1) Where dividend is declared but not paid or claimed within 30 days of declaration by a shareholder entitled to it, the company must, within 7 days of the expiry of those 30 days, transfer the total unpaid or unclaimed amount to a special account called the "Unpaid Dividend Account" in a scheduled bank.
(2) Within 90 days of the transfer, the company must prepare a statement of the names, last known addresses and unpaid dividend amounts of the persons concerned, and place it on its website (if any) and on any other website approved by the Central Government, as prescribed.
(3) If the company defaults in transferring the amount to the Unpaid Dividend Account, it must pay interest at 12% per annum from the date of default on the amount not transferred; the interest accrues to the shareholders in proportion to their unpaid dividend.
(4) Any person claiming to be entitled to an amount transferred under (1) may apply to the company for payment.
(5) Any money in the Unpaid Dividend Account that remains unpaid or unclaimed for 7 years from the date of transfer must be transferred by the company, with interest accrued, to the Investor Education and Protection Fund under section 125, with a statement in the prescribed form.
(6) All shares in respect of which dividend has not been paid or claimed for 7 consecutive years or more must be transferred by the company in the name of the Investor Education and Protection Fund, with a statement of prescribed details. A claimant of such shares may claim their transfer back from the Fund under the prescribed procedure. Explanation: if any dividend has been paid or claimed for any year within that 7-year period, the share is not transferred.
(7) If a company fails to comply with this section, it is liable to a penalty of ₹1 lakh plus ₹500 per day for continuing failure, up to ₹10 lakh; and every officer in default to a penalty of ₹25,000 plus ₹100 per day, up to ₹2 lakh (2020 amendment).

### Section 125. Investor Education and Protection Fund
(1) The Central Government must establish a fund called the Investor Education and Protection Fund (IEPF).
(2) The Fund is credited with:
  - (a) amounts given by the Central Government by way of grants after due appropriation by Parliament;
  - (b) donations by the Central Government, State Governments, companies or other institutions;
  - (c) amounts in the Unpaid Dividend Accounts transferred under section 124(5);
  - (d) amounts in the general revenue account of the Central Government transferred under section 205A(5) of the Companies Act, 1956 (as it stood before the 1999 amendment) and remaining unpaid or unclaimed on commencement of this Act;
  - (e) amounts lying in the IEPF under section 205C of the Companies Act, 1956;
  - (f) interest or other income from investments made from the Fund;
  - (g) amounts received under section 38(4);
  - (h) application money received by companies for allotment of securities and due for refund;
  - (i) matured deposits with companies other than banking companies;
  - (j) matured debentures with companies;
  - (k) interest accrued on amounts in (h) to (j);
  - (l) sale proceeds of fractional shares arising from bonus issues, mergers and amalgamations, for 7 or more years;
  - (m) redemption amount of preference shares remaining unpaid or unclaimed for 7 or more years; and
  - (n) such other amounts as may be prescribed.
  Amounts in (h) to (j) form part of the Fund only if they have remained unclaimed and unpaid for 7 years from the date they became due for payment.
(3) The Fund is utilised for:
  - (a) refund of unclaimed dividends, matured deposits, matured debentures, application money due for refund and interest thereon;
  - (b) promotion of investors' education, awareness and protection;
  - (c) distribution of any disgorged amount among eligible and identifiable applicants for shares or debentures, shareholders, debenture-holders or depositors who suffered losses from wrongful acts of any person, as ordered by the court that ordered disgorgement;
  - (d) reimbursement of legal expenses incurred by members, debenture-holders or depositors in pursuing class action suits under sections 37 and 245, as sanctioned by the Tribunal; and
  - (e) any other purpose incidental to the above, as prescribed.
(4) A person claiming a refund under (3)(a) may apply to the Authority constituted under (5) in the prescribed form.
(5) The Central Government must constitute, by notification, an Authority (IEPF Authority) to administer the Fund, consisting of a chairperson and such members (not exceeding 7 [verify]) and a chief executive officer as it may appoint.
(6) The Central Government may specify the terms and conditions of service of the chairperson, members and CEO.
(7) The Authority administers the Fund, maintains separate accounts and other records, and prepares an annual statement of accounts in the prescribed form, in consultation with the Comptroller and Auditor-General of India.
(8)–(10) The accounts of the Authority are audited by the Comptroller and Auditor-General, are forwarded with the audit report to the Central Government annually, and are laid before each House of Parliament; the Authority also prepares an annual report on its activities. [verify sub-section numbering]
(11) The Central Government may specify the manner of administration of the Fund and the procedure for refunds. [verify]

### Section 126. Right to dividend, rights shares and bonus shares to be held in abeyance pending registration of transfer of shares
Where an instrument of transfer of shares has been delivered to a company for registration and the transfer has not been registered, the company must, notwithstanding anything elsewhere in the Act:
  - (a) transfer the dividend relating to those shares to the Unpaid Dividend Account under section 124, unless the registered holder has authorised the company in writing to pay it to the transferee named in the instrument; and
  - (b) keep in abeyance, in relation to those shares, any offer of rights shares under section 62(1)(a) and any issue of fully paid-up bonus shares under the proviso to section 123(5).

### Section 127. Punishment for failure to distribute dividends
Where a dividend has been declared but has not been paid, or the warrant has not been posted, within 30 days of declaration to any shareholder entitled to it, every director who is knowingly a party to the default is punishable with imprisonment up to 2 years and fine of not less than ₹1,000 for every day of continuing default, and the company is liable to pay simple interest at 18% per annum during the period of default. No offence is committed:
  - (a) where the dividend could not be paid because of the operation of any law;
  - (b) where the shareholder gave directions about payment that could not be complied with, and this was communicated to him;
  - (c) where there is a dispute about the right to receive the dividend;
  - (d) where the dividend has been lawfully adjusted by the company against any sum due from the shareholder; or
  - (e) where, for any other reason, the failure was not due to default by the company.

## CHAPTER IX — ACCOUNTS OF COMPANIES

### Section 128. Books of account, etc., to be kept by company
(1) Every company must prepare and keep at its registered office books of account, other relevant books and papers, and financial statements for every financial year, giving a true and fair view of its affairs (including those of its branches) and explaining transactions at the registered office and branches. The books must be kept on accrual basis under the double entry system.
  - All or any of the books may be kept at another place in India decided by the Board; the company must within 7 days file with the Registrar a written notice giving the full address of that place.
  - The company may keep books of account and other papers in electronic mode as prescribed.
(2) Where a company has a branch office in India or outside, it is deemed to comply with (1) if proper books relating to the branch's transactions are kept at the branch and proper summarised returns are sent periodically to the registered office or other place under (1).
(3) The books of account and other books and papers (electronic or otherwise) are open to inspection by any director during business hours at the registered office or other place where kept. Financial information maintained outside India must have copies maintained and produced for inspection by any director, subject to prescribed conditions. Inspection relating to a subsidiary may be done only by a person authorised by Board resolution.
(4) Where an inspection is made under (3), the company's officers and employees must give the person making it all assistance in connection with the inspection that the company may reasonably be expected to give.
(5) Books of account and relevant vouchers must be preserved in good order for at least 8 financial years immediately preceding a financial year (or all preceding years if the company has existed for less than 8 years). Where an investigation has been ordered under Chapter XIV, the Central Government may direct that the books be kept for a longer period.
(6) If the managing director, whole-time director in charge of finance, Chief Financial Officer or any other person charged by the Board with the duty of complying with this section fails to take all reasonable steps to secure compliance, or by his own wilful act causes a default, he is punishable with imprisonment up to 1 year, or fine of not less than ₹50,000 extending to ₹5 lakh, or both. [verify]

### Section 129. Financial statement
(1) Financial statements must give a true and fair view of the state of affairs of the company or companies, comply with the accounting standards notified under section 133, and be in the form(s) provided in Schedule III. However:
  - this does not apply to an insurance or banking company, a company engaged in generation or supply of electricity, or any other class of company for which a form of financial statement is specified in or under the governing Act;
  - the financial statement need not disclose items that the governing Act does not require to be disclosed; and
  - such financial statement is not treated as failing to give a true and fair view merely because it does not disclose matters that the governing Act does not require to be disclosed.
(2) At every AGM, the Board must lay before the company the financial statements for the financial year.
(3) A company with one or more subsidiaries or associate companies must, in addition to (2), prepare a consolidated financial statement of the company and all its subsidiaries and associates in the same form and manner as its own, and lay it before the AGM together with the statement under (2). The company must also attach a separate statement containing the salient features of the financial statements of its subsidiaries in the prescribed form; the Central Government may prescribe the manner of consolidation. Explanation: "subsidiary" here includes associate company and joint venture.
(4) The provisions of the Act on preparation, adoption and audit of the holding company's financial statements apply, mutatis mutandis, to the consolidated financial statement.
(5) Without prejudice to (1), where financial statements do not comply with the accounting standards, the company must disclose the deviation, the reasons for it, and its financial effects.
(6) The Central Government may, on its own or on application by a class of companies, by order exempt any class of companies from any requirement of this section or the rules, if necessary in the public interest, with or without conditions.
(7) If a company contravenes this section, the managing director, whole-time director in charge of finance, Chief Financial Officer and any other person charged by the Board with the duty of compliance, and in their absence all directors, are punishable with imprisonment up to 1 year, or fine of not less than ₹50,000 extending to ₹5 lakh, or both.

### Section 129A. Periodical financial results
(Inserted by the Companies (Amendment) Act, 2020.) The Central Government may require such class or classes of unlisted companies as may be prescribed:
  - (a) to prepare their financial results on such periodical basis and in such form as may be prescribed;
  - (b) to obtain approval of the Board and complete an audit or limited review of such periodical financial results in the prescribed manner; and
  - (c) to file a copy with the Registrar within 30 days of completion of the relevant period, with prescribed fees.

### Section 130. Re-opening of accounts on court's or Tribunal's orders
(1) A company may not re-open its books of account or recast its financial statements unless a court of competent jurisdiction or the Tribunal so orders, on application by the Central Government, the Income-tax authorities, SEBI, any other statutory regulatory body or authority, or any person concerned, on the ground that:
  - (i) the relevant earlier accounts were prepared in a fraudulent manner; or
  - (ii) the company's affairs were mismanaged during the relevant period, casting doubt on the reliability of its financial statements.
  Before passing the order, the court or Tribunal must give notice to the Central Government, Income-tax authorities, SEBI or other concerned statutory body, and consider their representations.
(2) Accounts revised or recast under (1) are final.
(3) No order may be made under (1) to re-open books of account relating to a period earlier than 8 financial years immediately preceding the current financial year; but where the Central Government has directed under section 128(5) that books be kept for a longer period, the order may cover that longer period (2017 amendment).

### Section 131. Voluntary revision of financial statements or Board's report
(1) If it appears to the directors that:
  - (a) the financial statement of the company; or
  - (b) the report of the Board,
  does not comply with section 129 or section 134, they may prepare a revised financial statement or revised report for any of the 3 preceding financial years, after obtaining the Tribunal's approval on an application in the prescribed form and manner; a copy of the Tribunal's order must be filed with the Registrar. Provisos:
  - the Tribunal must give notice to the Central Government and the Income-tax authorities and consider their representations before passing the order;
  - the revised statement or report may not be prepared or filed more than once in a financial year;
  - the detailed reasons for revision must be disclosed in the Board's report for the year in which the revision is made.
(2) Where copies of the earlier statement or report have been sent to members, delivered to the Registrar or laid before a general meeting, the revisions must be confined to:
  - (a) correcting the respects in which the earlier version did not comply with sections 129 or 134; and
  - (b) making necessary consequential alterations.
(3) The Central Government may make rules on the application of the Act's provisions to revised financial statements or revised reports.

### Section 132. Constitution of National Financial Reporting Authority
(1) The Central Government must, by notification, constitute a National Financial Reporting Authority (NFRA) to provide for matters relating to accounting and auditing standards under this Act.
(1A) NFRA performs its functions through such divisions as may be prescribed (2019 amendment).
(2) Notwithstanding anything in any other law, NFRA must:
  - (a) make recommendations to the Central Government on formulation and laying down of accounting and auditing policies and standards for adoption by companies or classes of companies or their auditors;
  - (b) monitor and enforce compliance with accounting and auditing standards in the prescribed manner;
  - (c) oversee the quality of service of the professions associated with ensuring compliance with such standards, and suggest measures for improvement; and
  - (d) perform such other functions relating to (a) to (c) as may be prescribed.
(3) NFRA consists of a chairperson, who must be a person of eminence with expertise in accountancy, auditing, finance or law, appointed by the Central Government, and such other members, not exceeding 15, consisting of part-time and full-time members, as prescribed. The chairperson and members must declare that they have no conflict of interest or lack of independence; the chairperson and full-time members must not be associated with any audit firm (including related consultancy firms) during their appointment and for 2 years after ceasing to hold office.
(3A) Each division of NFRA is presided over by the chairperson or a full-time member authorised by the chairperson (2019 amendment).
(3B) There is an executive body of NFRA, consisting of the chairperson and full-time members, for efficient discharge of its functions under (2)(a) and (d) (2019 amendment).
(4) Notwithstanding anything in any other law, NFRA:
  - (a) may investigate, on its own motion or on reference by the Central Government, for prescribed classes of bodies corporate or persons, matters of professional or other misconduct committed by any member or firm of chartered accountants registered under the Chartered Accountants Act, 1949; where NFRA has initiated an investigation, no other institute or body may initiate or continue proceedings in the same matter;
  - (b) has the powers of a civil court under the Code of Civil Procedure, 1908 for: (i) discovery and production of books of account and other documents; (ii) summoning and enforcing attendance of persons and examining them on oath; (iii) inspection of books, registers and other documents of any person referred to in (a), at any place; and (iv) issuing commissions for examination of witnesses or documents;
  - (c) where professional or other misconduct is proved, may order:
    - (A) a penalty of — (I) not less than ₹1 lakh, extending to 5 times the fees received, for individuals; and (II) not less than ₹5 lakh, extending to 10 times the fees received, for firms; and
    - (B) debarment of the member or firm from practice as a member of the Institute of Chartered Accountants of India for a minimum of 6 months, or a higher period up to 10 years, as NFRA decides.
  Explanation: "professional or other misconduct" has the meaning given in section 22 of the Chartered Accountants Act, 1949.
(5) A person aggrieved by an NFRA order under (4)(c) may appeal to the Appellate Tribunal (NCLAT) in the prescribed manner and on payment of prescribed fees (2019 amendment, replacing the earlier separate Appellate Authority) [verify].
(6)–(10) [Provisions on a separate Appellate Authority — omitted/superseded by the 2019 amendment] [verify].
(11) NFRA may appoint a secretary and such other employees as it considers necessary.
(12) NFRA has its head office in New Delhi and may hold meetings at such places and times as it decides. [verify]
(13) NFRA must cause its books of account to be maintained and audited by the Comptroller and Auditor-General of India, and send its audited accounts to the Central Government, to be laid before each House of Parliament. [verify]
(14) NFRA must prepare an annual report giving a full account of its activities during the year and forward it to the Central Government, to be laid before each House of Parliament. [verify]
(15) The Central Government may, by notification, constitute a fund to be credited with grants, fees and penalties realised, and used for the expenses of NFRA. [verify]

### Section 133. Central Government to prescribe accounting standards
The Central Government may prescribe standards of accounting, or any addendum to them, as recommended by the Institute of Chartered Accountants of India, in consultation with and after examination of the recommendations made by the National Financial Reporting Authority.

### Section 134. Financial statement, Board's report, etc.
(1) The financial statement, including the consolidated financial statement, must be approved by the Board before being signed on its behalf — by the chairperson if authorised by the Board, or by two directors of whom one must be the managing director (if any), and by the Chief Executive Officer, Chief Financial Officer and Company Secretary wherever appointed — or, for a One Person Company, by one director only, for submission to the auditor for his report.
(2) The auditors' report must be attached to every financial statement.
(3) A Board's report must be attached to the statements laid before the general meeting, including:
  - (a) the web-link of the annual return referred to in section 92(3), if any (2017 amendment);
  - (b) the number of Board meetings;
  - (c) the Directors' Responsibility Statement;
  - (ca) details of frauds reported by auditors under section 143(12), other than those reportable to the Central Government (2015 amendment);
  - (d) a statement on the declarations by independent directors under section 149(6);
  - (e) for companies covered by section 178(1), the company's policy on directors' appointment and remuneration, including criteria for qualifications, positive attributes, independence and other matters under section 178(3);
  - (f) explanations or comments by the Board on every qualification, reservation, adverse remark or disclaimer made — (i) by the auditor in his report; and (ii) by the company secretary in practice in the secretarial audit report;
  - (g) particulars of loans, guarantees or investments under section 186;
  - (h) particulars of contracts or arrangements with related parties under section 188(1), in the prescribed form;
  - (i) the state of the company's affairs;
  - (j) the amount, if any, proposed to be carried to reserves;
  - (k) the amount, if any, recommended for payment as dividend;
  - (l) material changes and commitments, if any, affecting the company's financial position, occurring between the end of the financial year and the date of the report;
  - (m) conservation of energy, technology absorption, and foreign exchange earnings and outgo, in the prescribed manner;
  - (n) a statement on development and implementation of a risk management policy, including identification of risks that may threaten the company's existence;
  - (o) details of the policy developed and implemented on corporate social responsibility initiatives during the year;
  - (p) for a listed company and every other public company with prescribed paid-up capital, a statement on the manner of formal annual evaluation by the Board of its own performance and that of its committees and individual directors; and
  - (q) such other matters as may be prescribed.
  Provisos (2017 amendment): disclosures already included in the financial statements may be referred to rather than repeated in the Board's report; and where the policies in (e) or (o) are on the company's website, it is sufficient to give their salient features and any changes in brief in the Board's report, with the web address.
(3A) The Central Government may prescribe an abridged Board's report for a One Person Company or small company (2017 amendment).
(4) For a One Person Company, the Board's report means a report containing explanations or comments by the Board on every qualification, reservation, adverse remark or disclaimer made by the auditor in his report.
(5) The Directors' Responsibility Statement must state that:
  - (a) applicable accounting standards were followed in preparing the annual accounts, with proper explanation of material departures;
  - (b) the directors selected accounting policies, applied them consistently and made reasonable and prudent judgments and estimates, so as to give a true and fair view of the company's state of affairs at the end of the financial year and its profit and loss for that period;
  - (c) the directors took proper and sufficient care to maintain adequate accounting records under the Act, for safeguarding assets and preventing and detecting fraud and other irregularities;
  - (d) the directors prepared the annual accounts on a going concern basis;
  - (e) for a listed company, the directors laid down internal financial controls to be followed, and these are adequate and operating effectively. Explanation: "internal financial controls" means the policies and procedures adopted to ensure orderly and efficient conduct of business, including adherence to company policies, safeguarding of assets, prevention and detection of frauds and errors, accuracy and completeness of accounting records, and timely preparation of reliable financial information; and
  - (f) the directors devised proper systems to ensure compliance with all applicable laws, and these are adequate and operating effectively.
(6) The Board's report and its annexures must be signed by the chairperson if authorised by the Board, and otherwise by at least two directors, one of whom must be the managing director, or by the director where there is only one.
(7) A signed copy of every financial statement, including the consolidated financial statement, must be issued, circulated or published together with: (a) any notes annexed to or forming part of it; (b) the auditor's report; and (c) the Board's report under (3).
(8) If a company contravenes this section, the company is liable to a penalty of ₹3 lakh and every officer in default to a penalty of ₹50,000 (2019 amendment).

### Section 135. Corporate Social Responsibility
(1) Every company with net worth of ₹500 crore or more, or turnover of ₹1,000 crore or more, or net profit of ₹5 crore or more during the immediately preceding financial year (2019 amendment wording) must constitute a Corporate Social Responsibility Committee of the Board of 3 or more directors, at least one of whom is an independent director. Where the company is not required to appoint an independent director under section 149(4), the committee must have 2 or more directors.
(2) The Board's report under section 134(3) must disclose the composition of the CSR Committee.
(3) The CSR Committee must:
  - (a) formulate and recommend to the Board a CSR Policy indicating the activities to be undertaken, in the areas or subjects specified in Schedule VII;
  - (b) recommend the amount of expenditure on those activities; and
  - (c) monitor the CSR Policy from time to time.
(4) The Board of every company covered by (1) must:
  - (a) after considering the CSR Committee's recommendations, approve the CSR Policy, disclose its contents in the Board's report and place it on the company's website, if any, as prescribed; and
  - (b) ensure that the activities in the CSR Policy are undertaken by the company.
(5) The Board must ensure the company spends, in every financial year, at least 2% of its average net profits made during the 3 immediately preceding financial years (or, if it has not completed 3 years since incorporation, during the immediately preceding years), in pursuance of its CSR Policy. Provisos:
  - the company must give preference to the local area and areas around where it operates;
  - if the company fails to spend the amount, the Board must state the reasons in its report under section 134(3)(o) and, unless the unspent amount relates to an ongoing project under (6), transfer it to a Fund specified in Schedule VII within 6 months of the end of the financial year (2019 amendment);
  - if the company spends more than required, it may set off the excess against the requirement in such number of succeeding financial years and in such manner as prescribed (2020 amendment).
  Explanation: "net profit" excludes such sums as may be prescribed and is calculated in accordance with section 198.
(6) Any amount remaining unspent under (5) that relates to an ongoing project meeting prescribed conditions, undertaken under the CSR Policy, must be transferred within 30 days of the end of the financial year to a special account opened in a scheduled bank called the "Unspent Corporate Social Responsibility Account", and spent within 3 financial years from the date of transfer; failing which, it must be transferred to a Fund specified in Schedule VII within 30 days of completion of the third financial year (2019 amendment).
(7) If a company defaults in complying with (5) or (6), the company is liable to a penalty of twice the amount required to be transferred to the Schedule VII Fund or the Unspent CSR Account, or ₹1 crore, whichever is less; and every officer in default to a penalty of one-tenth of that amount or ₹2 lakh, whichever is less (2020 amendment, replacing the 2019 provision for imprisonment and fine).
(8) The Central Government may give general or special directions to a company or class of companies to ensure compliance with this section, and they must comply.
(9) Where the amount to be spent under (5) does not exceed ₹50 lakh, the requirement to constitute a CSR Committee does not apply, and the Committee's functions are discharged by the Board (2020 amendment).

### Section 136. Right of member to copies of audited financial statement
(1) Without prejudice to section 101, copies of the financial statements (including consolidated statements), the auditor's report and every other document required by law to be annexed or attached, laid before the general meeting, must be sent to every member, every trustee for debenture-holders, and every other person entitled, at least 21 days before the meeting. Provisos:
  - if sent less than 21 days before, they are deemed duly sent if so agreed by at least 90% of the members entitled to vote at the meeting (2017 amendment) [verify];
  - for listed companies (and other prescribed classes), the requirement is satisfied if the documents are made available by electronic mode or a statement of salient features is sent in the prescribed form, as prescribed [verify];
  - a company with subsidiaries must place the audited accounts of each subsidiary on its website, if any;
  - a listed company must place its financial statements (including consolidated statements) and all documents required to be attached on its website;
  - a company must provide a copy of the separate audited or unaudited financial statements of any subsidiary to any shareholder who asks;
  - a listed company with a foreign subsidiary that is not required by local law to be audited may place the unaudited financial statements of that subsidiary on its website, with an English translation where the statements are in another language (2017 amendment).
(2) The company must allow every member or trustee for debenture-holders to inspect the documents under (1) at its registered office during business hours.
(3) If default is made in complying with this section, the company is liable to a penalty of ₹25,000 and every officer in default to a penalty of ₹5,000 (2019 amendment).

### Section 137. Copy of financial statement to be filed with Registrar
(1) A copy of the financial statements (including consolidated statements, if any), with all documents required to be attached, duly adopted at the AGM, must be filed with the Registrar within 30 days of the AGM, in the prescribed manner with prescribed fees or additional fees within the time specified in section 403. Provisos:
  - if the statements are not adopted at the AGM or adjourned AGM, the unadopted statements with required documents must be filed within 30 days of the AGM, and the Registrar records them as provisional until the adopted statements are filed;
  - statements adopted at an adjourned AGM must be filed within 30 days of that adjourned meeting, with prescribed fees;
  - a One Person Company must file its financial statements, duly adopted by its member, with all required documents, within 180 days of the close of the financial year;
  - a company must attach the accounts of its subsidiaries incorporated outside India that have not established a place of business in India; where such a subsidiary is not required to be audited under local law, its unaudited statements may be filed with a declaration to that effect, with an English translation where the statements are in another language (2017 amendment) [verify].
(2) Where the AGM is not held in a year, the financial statements with required documents, duly signed, must be filed with the Registrar within 30 days of the last date by which the AGM should have been held, with a statement of facts and reasons for not holding it.
(3) If a company fails to file under (1) or (2) before the expiry of the period, the company is liable to a penalty of ₹10,000 plus ₹100 per day for continuing failure, up to ₹2 lakh; and the managing director and Chief Financial Officer (if any), or in their absence any other director charged by the Board with the responsibility, or in his absence all directors, are liable to a penalty of ₹10,000 plus ₹100 per day, up to ₹50,000 (2020 amendment).

### Section 138. Internal audit
(1) Such class or classes of companies as may be prescribed must appoint an internal auditor — a chartered accountant, cost accountant or such other professional as the Board decides — to conduct internal audit of the company's functions and activities. (Under the Rules, this covers every listed company; unlisted public companies with paid-up capital of ₹50 crore or more, turnover of ₹200 crore or more, outstanding bank/financial institution borrowings exceeding ₹100 crore, or outstanding deposits of ₹25 crore or more; and private companies with turnover of ₹200 crore or more or such borrowings exceeding ₹100 crore [verify].)
(2) The Central Government may by rules prescribe the manner and intervals of internal audit and reporting to the Board.

## CHAPTER X — AUDIT AND AUDITORS

### Section 139. Appointment of auditors
(1) Every company must, at its first AGM, appoint an individual or a firm as auditor, to hold office from the conclusion of that meeting until the conclusion of its sixth AGM, and thereafter until the conclusion of every sixth AGM; the manner and procedure of selection are as prescribed. (The requirement of ratification of the appointment by members at every AGM was omitted by the 2017 amendment.) Before the appointment, the auditor's written consent and a certificate must be obtained that the appointment, if made, is in accordance with prescribed conditions, and that the auditor satisfies the criteria in section 141. The company must inform the auditor and file a notice of the appointment with the Registrar within 15 days of the meeting. Explanation: "appointment" includes re-appointment.
(2) No listed company, or company of a prescribed class, may appoint or re-appoint:
  - (a) an individual as auditor for more than one term of 5 consecutive years; or
  - (b) an audit firm as auditor for more than two terms of 5 consecutive years.
  Provisos:
  - (i) an individual who has completed his term under (a) cannot be re-appointed as auditor of the same company for 5 years from completion of the term;
  - (ii) an audit firm that has completed its term under (b) cannot be re-appointed as auditor of the same company for 5 years from completion of the term;
  - no audit firm having a common partner with another audit firm whose tenure expired in the company in the immediately preceding financial year may be appointed as auditor of the same company for 5 years;
  - existing companies required to comply had 3 years from commencement of the Act to do so;
  - this sub-section does not prejudice the company's right to remove an auditor or the auditor's right to resign.
  Explanation I: periods of office held before commencement of the Act count for this sub-section. Explanation II: "same network" includes firms operating under the same brand name, trade name or common control.
  (Prescribed classes under the Rules: unlisted public companies with paid-up capital of ₹10 crore or more; private companies with paid-up capital of ₹50 crore or more; and all companies with public borrowings from financial institutions, banks or public deposits of ₹50 crore or more — excluding One Person Companies and small companies [verify].)
(3) Subject to (2), the members may resolve that:
  - (a) in the audit firm, the auditing partner and his team be rotated at intervals resolved by the members; or
  - (b) the audit be conducted by more than one auditor.
(4) The Central Government may by rules prescribe the manner of rotation of auditors under (2).
(5) Notwithstanding (1), for a Government company or any other company owned or controlled, directly or indirectly, by the Central Government, State Government(s), or partly by both, the Comptroller and Auditor-General of India must appoint a duly qualified auditor for a financial year within 180 days from the start of that year; the auditor holds office until the conclusion of the AGM.
(6) Notwithstanding (1), the first auditor of a company other than a Government company must be appointed by the Board within 30 days of registration; if the Board fails, it must inform the members, who must appoint the auditor within 90 days at an EGM; the auditor holds office until the conclusion of the first AGM.
(7) Notwithstanding (1) or (5), for a company covered by (5), the first auditor must be appointed by the Comptroller and Auditor-General within 60 days of registration; failing which the Board must appoint within the next 30 days; failing which the Board must inform the members, who must appoint within 60 days at an EGM; the auditor holds office until the conclusion of the first AGM.
(8) A casual vacancy in the office of auditor is filled:
  - (i) for a company other than one audited by a CAG-appointed auditor, by the Board within 30 days; but if the vacancy results from resignation, the appointment must also be approved at a general meeting convened within 3 months of the Board's recommendation, and the auditor holds office until the conclusion of the next AGM;
  - (ii) for a company audited by a CAG-appointed auditor, by the Comptroller and Auditor-General within 30 days; failing which by the Board within the next 30 days.
(9) Subject to (1) and the rules, a retiring auditor may be re-appointed at an AGM if:
  - (a) he is not disqualified for re-appointment;
  - (b) he has not given the company written notice of unwillingness to be re-appointed; and
  - (c) no special resolution has been passed at that meeting appointing someone else or expressly providing that he shall not be re-appointed.
(10) Where no auditor is appointed or re-appointed at an AGM, the existing auditor continues as auditor.
(11) Where a company must constitute an Audit Committee under section 177, all appointments of auditors, including filling of casual vacancies, must be made after taking into account the Committee's recommendations.

### Section 140. Removal, resignation of auditor and giving of special notice
(1) An auditor appointed under section 139 may be removed before the expiry of his term only by special resolution of the company, after obtaining the previous approval of the Central Government in the prescribed manner. The auditor must be given a reasonable opportunity of being heard before any action is taken.
(2) An auditor who resigns must, within 30 days of resignation, file a statement in the prescribed form with the company and the Registrar (and, for companies under section 139(5), also with the Comptroller and Auditor-General), indicating the reasons and other relevant facts.
(3) An auditor who fails to comply with (2) is liable to a penalty of ₹50,000 or an amount equal to his remuneration, whichever is less, plus ₹500 per day for continuing failure, up to ₹2 lakh (2020 amendment).
(4) Special notice is required for a resolution at an AGM appointing as auditor a person other than the retiring auditor, or expressly providing that a retiring auditor shall not be re-appointed, except where the retiring auditor has completed a consecutive tenure under section 139(2). Further:
  - (i) on receipt of notice of such a resolution, the company must promptly send a copy to the retiring auditor;
  - (ii) the retiring auditor may make written representations (not exceeding a reasonable length) and request that members be notified; unless received too late, the company must state in the notice of the resolution that representations have been made and send a copy to every member to whom notice of the meeting is sent;
  - (iii) if the copy is not sent because it was received too late or due to the company's default, the auditor may (without prejudice to his right to be heard orally) require that it be read out at the meeting; a copy must also be filed with the Registrar. The representation need not be sent or read out if the Tribunal, on application by the company or an aggrieved person, is satisfied that the rights are being abused to secure needless publicity for defamatory matter.
(5) Without prejudice to other provisions, the Tribunal, on its own motion or on application by the Central Government or any person concerned, may order the company to change its auditor if satisfied that the auditor has, directly or indirectly, acted fraudulently or abetted or colluded in any fraud by or in relation to the company or its directors or officers. Provisos:
  - if the application is by the Central Government and the Tribunal is satisfied that a change is required, it must within 15 days of receiving the application order that the auditor shall not function as auditor, and the Central Government may appoint another auditor in his place;
  - an auditor (individual or firm) against whom a final order is passed under this section is not eligible for appointment as auditor of any company for 5 years from the order, and is also liable to action under section 447.
  Explanation I: for a firm, liability falls on the firm and on every partner who acted fraudulently or abetted or colluded in the fraud. Explanation II: "auditor" includes a firm of auditors. (The 2019 amendment provides that proceedings under this sub-section continue, and the order applies, even if the auditor has resigned.) [verify]

### Section 141. Eligibility, qualifications and disqualifications of auditors
(1) A person is eligible for appointment as auditor only if he is a chartered accountant. A firm whose partners practising in India are mostly qualified for appointment may be appointed in its firm name.
(2) Where a firm, including a limited liability partnership, is appointed auditor, only partners who are chartered accountants may act and sign on behalf of the firm.
(3) The following are not eligible for appointment as auditor:
  - (a) a body corporate, other than a limited liability partnership registered under the Limited Liability Partnership Act, 2008;
  - (b) an officer or employee of the company;
  - (c) a person who is a partner, or in the employment, of an officer or employee of the company;
  - (d) a person who, or whose relative or partner:
    - (i) holds any security of or interest in the company, its subsidiary, its holding or associate company, or a subsidiary of such holding company — but a relative may hold securities or interest of face value up to ₹1,000 or such sum as prescribed (Rules: ₹1 lakh [verify]);
    - (ii) is indebted to the company, its subsidiary, its holding or associate company, or a subsidiary of such holding company, beyond the prescribed amount (Rules: ₹5 lakh [verify]); or
    - (iii) has given a guarantee or provided security for the indebtedness of any third person to the company, its subsidiary, its holding or associate company, or a subsidiary of such holding company, beyond the prescribed amount (Rules: ₹1 lakh [verify]);
  - (e) a person or firm having, directly or indirectly, a business relationship of the prescribed nature with the company, its subsidiary, its holding or associate company, or a subsidiary of such holding company or associate company;
  - (f) a person whose relative is a director, or is employed by the company as a director or key managerial personnel;
  - (g) a person in full-time employment elsewhere, or a person or a partner of a firm who, on the date of appointment or re-appointment, holds appointment as auditor of more than 20 companies other than One Person Companies, dormant companies, small companies and private companies with paid-up share capital below ₹100 crore (2017 amendment);
  - (h) a person convicted by a court of an offence involving fraud, where 10 years have not elapsed since the conviction;
  - (i) a person whose subsidiary, associate company or any other form of entity is engaged, on the date of appointment, in consulting and specialised services referred to in section 144.
  Explanation: "business relationship" is to be construed as prescribed.
(4) An auditor who becomes subject to any disqualification in (3) after appointment must vacate office, and the vacancy is treated as a casual vacancy.

### Section 142. Remuneration of auditors
(1) The auditor's remuneration is fixed in general meeting or in such manner as the general meeting determines. The Board may fix the remuneration of the first auditor appointed by it. For an auditor appointed by the Comptroller and Auditor-General, remuneration is fixed by the company in general meeting or as the general meeting determines (2017 amendment) [verify].
(2) The remuneration includes, besides the fee, expenses incurred by the auditor in connection with the audit and any facility extended to him, but excludes remuneration for any other service rendered at the company's request.

### Section 143. Powers and duties of auditors and auditing standards
(1) Every auditor has a right of access at all times to the company's books of account and vouchers, wherever kept, and may require from its officers such information and explanations as he considers necessary. Among other matters, he must inquire into:
  - (a) whether loans and advances made on the basis of security are properly secured, and whether their terms are prejudicial to the company or its members;
  - (b) whether transactions represented merely by book entries are prejudicial to the company;
  - (c) where the company is not an investment or banking company, whether assets consisting of shares, debentures and other securities have been sold at less than their purchase price;
  - (d) whether loans and advances have been shown as deposits;
  - (e) whether personal expenses have been charged to revenue account;
  - (f) where shares are stated in the books to have been allotted for cash, whether cash was actually received, and if not, whether the position stated in the books and balance sheet is correct, regular and not misleading.
  The auditor of a holding company also has a right of access to the records of its subsidiaries and associate companies insofar as they relate to consolidation of financial statements (2017 amendment).
(2) The auditor must report to the members on the accounts examined and on every financial statement required to be laid before the general meeting, stating, after considering the Act, the accounting and auditing standards and matters required to be included under the Act, rules or orders under (11), whether to the best of his information and knowledge the accounts and financial statements give a true and fair view of the company's state of affairs at the end of the financial year and of its profit or loss and cash flow for the year, and such other matters as may be prescribed.
(3) The auditor's report must also state:
  - (a) whether he sought and obtained all necessary information and explanations, and if not, the details and their effect on the financial statements;
  - (b) whether proper books of account have been kept as required by law, and proper returns adequate for audit received from branches he did not visit;
  - (c) whether the report on accounts of any branch audited under (8) by another person was sent to him and how he dealt with it;
  - (d) whether the balance sheet and profit and loss account agree with the books of account and returns;
  - (e) whether the financial statements comply with the accounting standards;
  - (f) his observations or comments on financial transactions or matters having any adverse effect on the company's functioning;
  - (g) whether any director is disqualified from appointment under section 164(2);
  - (h) any qualification, reservation or adverse remark relating to maintenance of accounts and connected matters;
  - (i) whether the company has adequate internal financial controls with reference to financial statements, and the operating effectiveness of such controls (2017 amendment); and
  - (j) such other matters as may be prescribed.
(4) Where any matter required to be included is answered in the negative or with a qualification, the report must state the reasons.
(5) For a company covered by section 139(5) or (7), the Comptroller and Auditor-General appoints the auditor and directs the manner of audit; the auditor must submit a copy of the audit report to the CAG, including the directions issued, action taken on them and their impact on the accounts and financial statements.
(6) Within 60 days of receiving the audit report under (5), the Comptroller and Auditor-General may:
  - (a) conduct a supplementary audit through persons he authorises, and require information or additional information from them; and
  - (b) comment on or supplement the audit report.
  Any such comments or supplement must be sent by the company to every person entitled to copies of audited financial statements under section 136(1), and placed before the AGM at the same time and in the same manner as the audit report.
(7) Without prejudice to the above, the Comptroller and Auditor-General may, for a company covered by section 139(5) or (7), order a test audit of its accounts if he considers it necessary; section 19A of the Comptroller and Auditor-General's (Duties, Powers and Conditions of Service) Act, 1971 applies to the test audit report.
(8) A branch office's accounts are audited either by the company's auditor or by another person qualified under section 139; for a branch outside India, by the company's auditor or by an accountant or other person qualified to audit under that country's laws. The branch auditor's duties and powers are as prescribed; he must prepare a report on the branch accounts and send it to the company's auditor, who deals with it in his own report as he thinks necessary.
(9) Every auditor must comply with the auditing standards.
(10) The Central Government may prescribe auditing standards, or any addendum, as recommended by the Institute of Chartered Accountants of India, in consultation with and after examination of the recommendations of NFRA. Until such standards are notified, the standards specified by the Institute are deemed to be the auditing standards.
(11) The Central Government may, in consultation with NFRA, by general or special order, direct that the auditor's report for specified classes of companies include a statement on specified matters (e.g., the Companies (Auditor's Report) Order).
(12) Notwithstanding anything in this section, if an auditor, in the course of his duties, has reason to believe that an offence of fraud involving the prescribed amount or more (Rules: ₹1 crore [verify]) is being or has been committed against the company by its officers or employees, he must report it to the Central Government within the prescribed time and manner. Frauds involving lesser amounts must be reported to the Audit Committee under section 177, or to the Board in other cases, within the prescribed time and manner; companies whose auditors have so reported frauds (not reportable to the Central Government) must disclose the details in the Board's report as prescribed (2015 amendment).
(13) An auditor who reports a matter under (12) in good faith is not treated as having breached any duty to which he is subject.
(14) This section applies, mutatis mutandis, to:
  - (a) a cost accountant conducting cost audit under section 148; and
  - (b) a company secretary in practice conducting secretarial audit under section 204.
(15) An auditor, cost accountant or company secretary in practice who fails to comply with (12) is liable to a penalty of — (a) ₹5 lakh for a listed company; and (b) ₹1 lakh for any other company (2019 amendment).

### Section 144. Auditor not to render certain services
An auditor appointed under the Act may provide to the company only such other services as are approved by the Board or the Audit Committee, and may not render any of the following services, directly or indirectly, to the company, its holding company or its subsidiary company:
  - (a) accounting and book keeping services;
  - (b) internal audit;
  - (c) design and implementation of any financial information system;
  - (d) actuarial services;
  - (e) investment advisory services;
  - (f) investment banking services;
  - (g) rendering of outsourced financial services;
  - (h) management services; and
  - (i) any other kind of services as may be prescribed.
An auditor or audit firm providing such non-audit services on or before commencement of the Act had to comply before the close of the first financial year after commencement.
Explanation: "directly or indirectly" includes services rendered:
  - (i) for an individual — by himself, through his relative or any other person connected or associated with him, or through any entity in which he has significant influence or control or whose name, trade mark or brand he uses;
  - (ii) for a firm — by itself, through any of its partners, through its parent, subsidiary or associate entity, or through any entity in which the firm or any partner has significant influence or control or whose name, trade mark or brand the firm or any partner uses.

### Section 145. Auditor to sign audit reports, etc.
The person appointed as auditor must sign the auditor's report, and sign or certify any other company document, in accordance with section 141(2). Qualifications, observations or comments on financial transactions or matters having any adverse effect on the company's functioning that are mentioned in the auditor's report must be read before the company in general meeting and be open to inspection by any member.

### Section 146. Auditors to attend general meeting
All notices of, and other communications relating to, any general meeting must be forwarded to the auditor. Unless exempted by the company, the auditor must attend every general meeting, personally or through an authorised representative who is also qualified to be an auditor, and has the right to be heard at the meeting on any part of the business that concerns him as auditor.

### Section 147. Punishment for contravention
(1) If any of sections 139 to 146 (both inclusive) is contravened, the company is punishable with fine of not less than ₹25,000, extending to ₹5 lakh, and every officer in default is punishable with fine of not less than ₹10,000, extending to ₹1 lakh [the earlier alternative of imprisonment up to 1 year for officers was removed by the 2020 amendment — verify].
(2) If an auditor contravenes any of sections 139, 143, 144 or 145, he is punishable with fine of not less than ₹25,000, extending to ₹5 lakh or four times his remuneration, whichever is less. If he did so knowingly or wilfully with intent to deceive the company, its shareholders, creditors or tax authorities, he is punishable with imprisonment up to 1 year and fine of not less than ₹50,000, extending to ₹25 lakh or eight times his remuneration, whichever is less.
(3) An auditor convicted under (2) must:
  - (i) refund the remuneration received to the company; and
  - (ii) pay damages to the company, statutory bodies or authorities, or members or creditors, for loss arising from incorrect or misleading statements in his audit report.
(4) The Central Government must, by notification, specify a statutory body, authority or officer to ensure prompt payment of damages under (3)(ii); after payment, that body must file a report with the Central Government as specified.
(5) Where an audit is conducted by an audit firm and it is proved that a partner or partners acted fraudulently or abetted or colluded in fraud by, in relation to, or by the company or its directors or officers, civil or criminal liability under the Act or any other law falls jointly and severally on the partner(s) concerned and the firm. For criminal liability other than fine, only the partner(s) who acted fraudulently or abetted or colluded are liable (2017 amendment).

### Section 148. Central Government to specify audit of items of cost in respect of certain companies
(1) Notwithstanding anything in this Chapter, the Central Government may, by order, direct that for such class of companies engaged in production of such goods or provision of such services as prescribed, particulars relating to utilisation of material or labour or other items of cost, as prescribed, be included in their books of account. For a class of companies regulated under a special Act, the Government must first consult the regulatory body under that Act.
(2) If the Central Government considers it necessary, having regard to the nature of business, it may by order direct that an audit of cost records be conducted, in the manner specified, for a class of companies covered by (1) having net worth or turnover of the prescribed amount; again, prior consultation with the regulator under any special Act is required.
(3) The cost audit is conducted by a Cost Accountant appointed by the Board, on remuneration determined by the members as prescribed. A person appointed as auditor under section 139 may not be appointed to audit cost records. The cost auditor must comply with the cost auditing standards. Explanation: "cost auditing standards" means standards issued by the Institute of Cost and Works Accountants of India (constituted under the Cost and Works Accountants Act, 1959) with the Central Government's approval.
(4) The cost audit is in addition to the audit under section 143.
