It may do so where circumstances suggest that:
  - (a) a person concerned in conducting and managing the company's affairs is or has been guilty of **fraud, misfeasance, persistent negligence**, default in carrying out their legal obligations and functions, or **breach of trust**;
  - (b) the business is not, or has not been, run by that person in line with **sound business principles or prudent commercial practices**;
  - (c) the company is or has been run by that person in a way that causes, or is likely to cause, **serious injury or damage** to the trade, industry or business it belongs to; or
  - (d) the business is or has been run by that person with intent to **defraud** its creditors, members or any other person, or for a fraudulent or unlawful purpose, or in a manner prejudicial to the public interest.
- (4) The person against whom a case is referred under sub-section (3) must be made a **respondent** to the application.
- (5) Every application under sub-section (3) must:
  - (a) contain a concise statement of the circumstances and materials the Central Government considers necessary for the inquiry; and
  - (b) be signed and verified in the way the Code of Civil Procedure, 1908 requires for signing and verifying a plaint, as if the Central Government were the plaintiff.

### Section 242. Powers of Tribunal
- (1) The Tribunal may make any order it thinks fit to bring an end to the matters complained of, if, on an application under section 241, it is of the opinion that:
  - (a) the company's affairs have been or are being conducted in a manner prejudicial or oppressive to any member or members, or prejudicial to the public interest or to the company's interests; and
  - (b) winding up the company would **unfairly prejudice** those members, but the facts would otherwise justify a winding-up order on the ground that it is **just and equitable**.
- (2) Without limiting sub-section (1), the order may provide for:
  - (a) regulating how the company's affairs are conducted in future;
  - (b) the **purchase of shares** or interests of any members by other members or by the company;
  - (c) where the company buys its own shares, the resulting **reduction of share capital**;
  - (d) restrictions on transferring or allotting the company's shares;
  - (e) ending, setting aside or modifying any agreement, however made, between the company and its managing director, any other director or the manager, on terms the Tribunal thinks just and equitable;
  - (f) ending, setting aside or modifying any agreement between the company and any other person;
    - Proviso: Such an agreement may be ended, set aside or modified only after **due notice** to the party concerned and with that party's **consent**;
  - (g) setting aside any transfer, delivery of goods, payment, execution or other act relating to property, made or done by or against the company within **three months** before the application, if it would count as a **fraudulent preference** had it been made or done by or against an individual in insolvency;
  - (h) **removing** the managing director, the manager or any of the directors;
  - (i) **recovering undue gains** made by any managing director, manager or director during their appointment, and deciding how the recovered amount is used, including transfer to the **Investor Education and Protection Fund** or repayment to identifiable victims;
  - (j) how a managing director or manager may be appointed after a removal under clause (h);
  - (k) appointing as many persons as directors as the Tribunal requires, to report to it on matters it directs;
  - (l) imposing such **costs** as the Tribunal thinks fit; and
  - (m) any other matter for which the Tribunal thinks it just and equitable to provide.
- (3) The company must file a certified copy of the Tribunal's order under sub-section (1) with the **Registrar within 30 days** of the order.
- (4) On the application of any party to the proceeding, the Tribunal may make any **interim order** it thinks fit to regulate the conduct of the company's affairs, on just and equitable terms.
- (4A) (Inserted 2017) At the end of the hearing of a case referred under section 241(3), the Tribunal must record its decision, stating specifically whether or not the respondent is a **fit and proper person** to hold the office of director or any other office connected with the conduct and management of any company.
- (5) If the Tribunal's order alters the company's memorandum or articles, the company may not make any inconsistent alteration to them without the **leave of the Tribunal**, except to the extent the order allows.
- (6) Subject to sub-section (1), alterations made by the order have the same effect in all respects as if the company had duly made them under the Act. The Act's provisions then apply to the memorandum or articles as altered.
- (7) A certified copy of every order that alters, or gives leave to alter, the memorandum or articles must be filed by the company with the **Registrar within 30 days**, and the Registrar must register it.
- (8) If the company contravenes sub-section (5):
  - the **company** is punishable with a **fine of ₹1 lakh to ₹25 lakh**; and
  - **every officer in default** is punishable with **imprisonment of up to 6 months**, or a **fine of ₹25,000 to ₹1 lakh**, or both `[verify]`.

### Section 243. Consequence of termination or modification of certain agreements
- (1) Where an order under section 242 ends, sets aside or modifies an agreement of the kind in section 242(2)(e):
  - (a) the order gives no one any claim against the company for **damages** or **compensation for loss of office**, or for anything else, whether under the agreement or otherwise; and
  - (b) the managing director, other director or manager whose agreement was ended or set aside may not, for **five years** from the date of the order, be appointed or act as managing director, other director or manager of the company without the **Tribunal's leave**.
    - Proviso: The Tribunal may not grant leave unless notice of the intended application has been served on the **Central Government** and it has had a reasonable opportunity to be heard.
- (1A) (Inserted 2017) A person found **not fit and proper** under section 242(4A) may not hold the office of director, or any other office connected with the conduct and management of any company, for **five years** from the decision.
  - Proviso: The Central Government may, with the **leave of the Tribunal**, allow the person to hold such an office before the five years end.
- (2) The following are punishable with a **fine of up to ₹5 lakh**:
  - a person who **knowingly** acts as managing director, other director or manager in breach of clause (b) of sub-section (1) or sub-section (1A); and
  - every other director who is **knowingly** a party to the breach.

### Section 244. Right to apply under section 241
- (1) The following members may apply under section 241:
  - (a) in a company **with share capital**: whichever is less of **100 members** or **one-tenth of the total number of members**; or any member or members holding **one-tenth of the issued share capital**. In each case the applicants must have paid all calls and other sums due on their shares;
  - (b) in a company **without share capital**: at least **one-fifth of the total number of members**.
  - Proviso: The Tribunal may, on an application, **waive** all or any of the requirements in clause (a) or (b), so that the members can apply under section 241.
  - Explanation: Where shares are held **jointly** by two or more persons, they count as **one member**.
- (2) Where some members are entitled to apply under sub-section (1), one or more of them may apply on behalf of, and for the benefit of, all of them, with the **written consent** of the others.

### Section 245. Class action
- (1) Members or depositors (or any class of them), in the numbers set out in sub-section (3), may apply to the Tribunal on behalf of the members or depositors if they believe the company's affairs are being managed or conducted in a way prejudicial to the interests of the company, its members or its depositors. They may seek all or any of these orders:
  - (a) restraining the company from doing an act that is **ultra vires** its articles or memorandum;
  - (b) restraining the company from **breaching** any provision of its memorandum or articles;
  - (c) declaring **void** a resolution altering the memorandum or articles, if it was passed by suppressing material facts or by misstatement to the members or depositors;
  - (d) restraining the company and its directors from acting on such a resolution;
  - (e) restraining the company from doing anything **contrary to the Act** or any other law in force;
  - (f) restraining the company from acting **contrary to a resolution** passed by the members;
  - (g) claiming **damages or compensation**, or other suitable action, from or against:
    - (i) the company or its directors, for any fraudulent, unlawful or wrongful act, omission or conduct, or any likely act, omission or conduct;
    - (ii) the **auditor**, including the **audit firm**, for any improper or misleading statement of particulars in the audit report, or for any fraudulent, unlawful or wrongful act or conduct; or
    - (iii) any **expert, adviser, consultant** or other person, for any incorrect or misleading statement made to the company, or for any fraudulent, unlawful or wrongful act or conduct, or any likely act or conduct; and
  - (h) any other remedy the Tribunal thinks fit.
- (2) Where damages, compensation or other action is sought against an **audit firm**, the liability falls on the **firm** and on **each partner** who:
  - was involved in making the improper or misleading statement in the audit report; or
  - acted in a fraudulent, unlawful or wrongful manner.
- (3) The required numbers are:
  - (i) **members**:
    - (a) in a company **with share capital**: whichever is less of **at least 100 members** or a prescribed percentage of the total number of members; or any member or members holding a prescribed percentage of the issued share capital. The applicants must have paid all calls and other sums due on their shares;
    - (b) in a company **without share capital**: at least **one-fifth of the total number of members**;
    - Under Rule 84 of the NCLT Rules, 2016, the prescribed thresholds are **5% of total members** and **2% of issued share capital** `[verify]`;
  - (ii) **depositors**: whichever is less of **at least 100 depositors** or a prescribed percentage of the total number of depositors (**5%** `[verify]`); or any depositor or depositors to whom the company owes a prescribed percentage of its total deposits (**5%** `[verify]`).
- (4) In considering the application, the Tribunal must take into account in particular:
  - (a) whether the member or depositor is acting in **good faith**;
  - (b) any evidence that persons other than the company's directors or officers were involved in the matters in clauses (a) to (f) of sub-section (1);
  - (c) whether the member or depositor could pursue the cause of action **in their own right** rather than through an order under this section;
  - (d) any evidence of the views of members or depositors who have **no personal interest**, direct or indirect, in the matter;
  - (e) where the act or omission is yet to happen, whether it could be, and in the circumstances is likely to be:
    - (i) authorised by the company before it happens; or
    - (ii) ratified by the company after it happens; and
  - (f) where the act or omission has already happened, whether it could be, and in the circumstances is likely to be, **ratified** by the company.
- (5) If the application is admitted, the Tribunal must ensure that:
  - (a) a **public notice** of the admission is served on all members or depositors of the class, in the prescribed manner;
  - (b) all similar applications in any jurisdiction are **consolidated** into a single application. The class members or depositors may choose a **lead applicant**; if they cannot agree, the Tribunal may appoint one, who will be in charge of the proceedings for the applicants;
  - (c) two class action applications on the **same cause of action** are not allowed; and
  - (d) the costs or expenses of the class action are paid by the company or by any other person responsible for the oppressive act.
- (6) Any order passed by the Tribunal is **binding** on:
  - the company and all its members and depositors;
  - its auditor, including the audit firm; and
  - any expert, consultant, adviser or other person associated with the company.
- (7) If a company fails to comply with an order under this section:
  - the **company** is punishable with a **fine of ₹5 lakh to ₹25 lakh**; and
  - **every officer in default** is punishable with **imprisonment of up to three years** and a **fine of ₹25,000 to ₹1 lakh**.
- (8) If an application is found to be **frivolous or vexatious**, the Tribunal must reject it, for reasons recorded in writing, and order the applicant to pay the opposite party costs of **up to ₹1 lakh**, as specified in the order.
- (9) This section does not apply to a **banking company**.
- (10) Subject to this section, an application may be filed, or other action taken, by any person, group of persons or association of persons representing those affected by any act or omission referred to in sub-section (1).

### Section 246. Application of certain provisions to proceedings under section 241 or section 245
- **Sections 337 to 341** (both included) apply, with the necessary changes, to applications made to the Tribunal under **section 241** or **section 245**. These sections deal with:
  - fraud and misconduct by officers;
  - liability where proper accounts are not kept;
  - fraudulent conduct of business; and
  - the Tribunal's power to assess damages against delinquent directors and others.

## CHAPTER XVII — REGISTERED VALUERS

### Section 247. Valuation by registered valuers
- (1) Where the Act requires a valuation of any property, stocks, shares, debentures, securities, goodwill or other assets, or of the company's net worth or liabilities, it must be done by a **registered valuer** who:
  - has the prescribed qualifications and experience;
  - is registered as a valuer, and is a member of a recognised organisation, as prescribed; and
  - is appointed by the **Audit Committee**, or, if there is none, by the **Board of Directors**.
  - The valuation is done in the prescribed manner and on the prescribed terms and conditions. Under the Companies (Registered Valuers and Valuation) Rules, 2017, the IBBI is the authority.
- (2) The valuer must:
  - (a) make an **impartial, true and fair** valuation;
  - (b) exercise **due diligence**;
  - (c) value in line with the prescribed rules; and
  - (d) not value any asset in which they have a **direct or indirect interest**, or in which they become interested at any time during the **three years before** their appointment or the **three years after** the valuation.
- (3) A valuer who contravenes this section or its rules is punishable with a **fine of ₹25,000 to ₹1 lakh**.
  - Proviso: If the valuer acted with **intent to defraud** the company or its members, they are punishable with **imprisonment of up to one year** and a **fine of ₹1 lakh to ₹5 lakh** `[verify]`.
- (4) A valuer convicted under sub-section (3) must:
  - (a) **refund** to the company the remuneration they received; and
  - (b) pay **damages** to the company, or to any other person, for loss caused by incorrect or misleading statements in their report.

## CHAPTER XVIII — REMOVAL OF NAMES OF COMPANIES FROM THE REGISTER OF COMPANIES

### Section 248. Power of Registrar to remove name of company from register of companies
- (1) The Registrar may act under this sub-section if they have **reasonable cause to believe** that:
  - (a) the company has **failed to commence business** within **one year** of incorporation;
  - (b) `[Omitted]` `[verify]`;
  - (c) the company has not carried on any business or operation for the **two immediately preceding financial years**, and has not applied in that period for **dormant company** status under section 455;
  - (d) (inserted 2019) the subscribers to the memorandum have not paid the subscription they undertook to pay at incorporation, and the declaration under **section 10A(1)** has not been filed within **180 days** of incorporation; or
  - (e) (inserted 2019) a **physical verification** under section 12(9) has shown that the company is not carrying on any business or operations.
  - The Registrar must send a notice to the company and all its directors stating the intention to **remove the company's name** from the register. The notice asks them to send representations, with copies of relevant documents, within **30 days** of the notice.
- (2) Without limiting sub-section (1), a company may itself apply to the Registrar, in the prescribed manner (Form STK-2), to remove its name on all or any of the grounds in sub-section (1). The conditions are:
  - the company must first **extinguish all its liabilities**; and
  - the application must be backed by a **special resolution**, or by the consent of **75% of the members in terms of paid-up share capital**.
  - On receiving the application, the Registrar must issue a **public notice** in the prescribed manner. Such applications are processed centrally by the Centre for Processing Accelerated Corporate Exit (C-PACE) `[verify]`.
- (3) Sub-section (2) does not apply to a company registered under **section 8**.
- (4) A notice under sub-section (1) or (2) must be published in the prescribed manner and also in the **Official Gazette** for public information.
- (5) When the time in the notice expires, the Registrar may **strike off** the company's name from the register, unless the company shows cause otherwise. The Registrar must publish a notice of the striking off in the Official Gazette. On that publication, the company stands **dissolved**.
- (6) Before passing an order under sub-section (5), the Registrar must be satisfied that sufficient provision has been made for:
  - realising all amounts due to the company; and
  - paying or discharging its liabilities and obligations within a reasonable time.
  - Where necessary, the Registrar must take **undertakings** from the managing director, directors or other persons in charge of management.
  - Proviso: Despite those undertakings, the company's assets remain available to pay or discharge all its liabilities and obligations even after its name is removed.
- (7) The **liability** of every director, manager or other officer who exercised any power of management, and of every member, of a company dissolved under sub-section (5) **continues** and may be enforced as if the company had not been dissolved.
- (8) This section does not affect the **Tribunal's power to wind up** a company whose name has been struck off.

### Section 249. Restrictions on making application under section 248 in certain situations
- (1) A company may not apply under section 248(2) if, at any time in the previous **three months**, it has:
  - (a) **changed its name**, or moved its registered office from one State to another;
  - (b) disposed of, for value, any property or rights it held immediately before it stopped trading or doing business, other than for gain in the normal course of trading or business;
  - (c) engaged in any other activity, except what is necessary or expedient to:
    - make the application, or decide whether to make it;
    - conclude the company's affairs; or
    - comply with any statutory requirement;
  - (d) applied to the Tribunal for sanction of a **compromise or arrangement**, and the matter is not yet finally concluded; or
  - (e) been **wound up** under Chapter XX of this Act or under the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 2016.
- (2) An application under section 248(2) must be **withdrawn** by the company, or **rejected** by the Registrar, as soon as possible after the company breaches any condition in sub-section (1).
- (3) A company that applies in breach of sub-section (1) is punishable with a **fine of up to ₹1 lakh** `[verify]`.

### Section 250. Effect of company notified as dissolved
- When a company is dissolved under section 248, from the date stated in the notice under section 248(5):
  - it **ceases to operate** as a company; and
  - its **Certificate of Incorporation** is treated as **cancelled**.
- The only exceptions are realising amounts due to the company and paying or discharging its liabilities or obligations.

### Section 251. Fraudulent application for removal of name
- (1) If a company applied under section 248(2) to **evade its liabilities**, or to **deceive its creditors** or defraud any other person, the persons in charge of its management, even though the company has been notified as dissolved:
  - (a) are **jointly and severally liable** to anyone who suffered loss or damage because the company was notified as dissolved; and
  - (b) are punishable for **fraud** under section 447.
- (2) Without limiting sub-section (1), the Registrar may also recommend **prosecution** of the persons responsible for the application.

### Section 252. Appeal to Tribunal
- (1) A person aggrieved by the Registrar's order notifying a company as dissolved under section 248 may **appeal to the Tribunal** within **three years** of the order.
  - If the Tribunal finds the removal **not justified**, because none of the grounds for the order existed, it may order the company's name to be **restored** to the register.
  - Proviso: Before passing an order, the Tribunal must give the Registrar, the company and all persons concerned a reasonable opportunity to make representations and be heard.
  - Proviso (2017): If the Registrar is satisfied that a company's name was struck off **inadvertently**, or on the basis of **incorrect information** furnished by the company or its directors, the Registrar may apply to the Tribunal within **three years** of the dissolution order to have the name restored.
- (2) The company must file a copy of the Tribunal's order with the **Registrar within 30 days** of the order. On receiving it, the Registrar must restore the company's name to the register and issue a **fresh certificate of incorporation**.
- (3) The company, or any of its members, creditors or workmen, may apply to the Tribunal if aggrieved that the company's name was struck off. The application must be made before **20 years** have passed since the notice under section 248(5) was published in the Official Gazette.
  - The Tribunal may order the name to be **restored** if satisfied that the company was carrying on business or was in operation when it was struck off, or that it is otherwise **just** to restore it.
  - The Tribunal may also give directions and make provisions it thinks just to put the company and all other persons, as nearly as possible, in the position they would have been in if the name had not been struck off.

## CHAPTER XX — WINDING UP

### PART I — WINDING UP BY THE TRIBUNAL

### Section 270. Winding up by Tribunal
(Substituted by the IBC, 2016, Eleventh Schedule [verify: w.e.f. 15-11-2016].)
Part I of this Chapter governs the winding up of a company by the Tribunal under this Act.
(The original section described two modes of winding up: by the Tribunal or voluntarily. Voluntary winding up of companies now falls under the IBC, 2016, Part II, section 59.)

### Section 271. Circumstances in which company may be wound up by Tribunal
(Substituted by the IBC, 2016.)
On a petition under section 272, the Tribunal may wind up a company in any of these cases:
- (a) the company has passed a special resolution that the Tribunal should wind it up;
- (b) the company has acted against the interests of the sovereignty and integrity of India, the security of the State, friendly relations with foreign States, public order, decency or morality;
- (c) the Registrar, or any other person authorised by the Central Government by notification under this Act, has applied, and the Tribunal is of the opinion that it is proper to wind the company up because:
  - the company's affairs have been conducted fraudulently;
  - the company was formed for a fraudulent and unlawful purpose; or
  - the persons involved in forming or managing it have been guilty of fraud, misfeasance or misconduct in that connection;
- (d) the company has defaulted in filing its financial statements or annual returns with the Registrar for the immediately preceding five consecutive financial years; or
- (e) the Tribunal considers it just and equitable that the company be wound up.

(The former ground of the company being "unable to pay its debts", and the related deeming provisions, were removed in 2016. Insolvency is now dealt with under the IBC, 2016.)

### Section 272. Petition for winding up
(1) Subject to this section, any of the following may present a petition to the Tribunal for winding up a company:
- (a) the company;
- (b) any contributory or contributories;
- (c) all or any of the persons in clauses (a) and (b);
- (d) the Registrar;
- (e) any person authorised by the Central Government in that behalf; or
- (f) in a case under section 271(b), the Central Government or a State Government.

[verify: the clause allowing creditors to petition, and the related creditor sub-section, were omitted by the IBC, 2016.]

(2) A contributory may present a petition even if he holds fully paid-up shares, or if the company has no assets at all, or has no surplus assets left for distribution among shareholders after its liabilities are met. A contributory's shares count for this purpose if they were:
- originally allotted to him;
- held by him and registered in his name for at least six months during the eighteen months immediately before the winding up began; or
- devolved on him through the death of a former holder [verify].

(3) [verify: omitted / modified by the IBC, 2016.]

(4) The Registrar may present a petition on any ground in section 271, but only with the Central Government's previous sanction. The Central Government must not grant that sanction until the company has had a reasonable opportunity to make representations.

(5) A petition presented by the company must be filed together with a statement of affairs in the prescribed form and manner.

(6) A copy of the petition must also be filed with the Registrar. Without prejudice to anything else in this section, the Registrar must give the Tribunal his views on the petition within sixty days of receiving it.

### Section 273. Powers of Tribunal
(1) When the Tribunal receives a winding up petition under section 272, it may:
- (a) dismiss the petition, with or without costs;
- (b) make any interim order it thinks fit;
- (c) appoint a provisional liquidator for the company until a winding up order is made;
- (d) order the company to be wound up, with or without costs; or
- (e) make any other order it thinks fit.

Provisos:
- The Tribunal must make an order under this sub-section within ninety days from the date the petition is presented.
- Before appointing a provisional liquidator, the Tribunal must give the company notice and a reasonable opportunity to make representations. It may skip this only for special reasons, which it must record in writing.
- The Tribunal may not refuse a winding up order merely because the company's assets are mortgaged for an amount equal to or greater than the assets, or because the company has no assets.

(2) Where the petition is based on the just and equitable ground, the Tribunal may refuse a winding up order if it thinks the petitioners have another remedy available and are acting unreasonably in seeking winding up instead of pursuing that remedy.

### Section 274. Directions for filing statement of affairs
(1) Where a person other than the company files the petition, and the Tribunal is satisfied that a prima facie case for winding up exists, it must direct the company to file its objections, together with a statement of its affairs. These must be filed within thirty days of the order, in the prescribed form and manner. In a contingency, the Tribunal may allow a further period of up to thirty days. The Tribunal may also direct the petitioner to deposit security for costs as a condition of issuing this direction.

(2) If the company does not file the statement of affairs, it loses its right to oppose the petition. Its directors and officers in default become liable for punishment under sub-section (4).

(3) Within thirty days of a winding up order, the directors and other officers of the company must, at their own cost, give the Company Liquidator the company's books of account. These must be completed and audited up to the date of the order.

(4) A director or officer who contravenes this section is punishable with:
- imprisonment up to six months; or
- a fine of not less than twenty-five thousand rupees, which may extend to five lakh rupees; or
- both [verify].

### Section 275. Company Liquidators and their appointments
(1) When passing a winding up order, the Tribunal must appoint as Company Liquidator either an Official Liquidator or a liquidator from the panel kept under sub-section (2).

(2) The provisional liquidator or Company Liquidator is to be appointed from a panel kept by the Central Government. The panel consists of the names of insolvency professionals registered under the IBC, 2016. (Substituted by the IBC, 2016. The earlier panel comprised chartered accountants, advocates, company secretaries, cost accountants, and firms or bodies corporate of such professionals, with at least ten years' experience in company matters [verify].)

(3) The Tribunal fixes the terms and conditions of appointment of a provisional liquidator or Company Liquidator, and the fee payable to him. In doing so, it takes into account the nature of the work, the liquidator's experience, qualifications and the size of the company.

(4) An appointee must file a declaration with the Tribunal in the prescribed form within seven days of appointment. The declaration discloses any conflict of interest or lack of independence regarding the appointment. This obligation continues throughout the term of appointment.

(5) When passing an order appointing a provisional liquidator, the Tribunal may limit and restrict his powers by that order or a later order. Otherwise, he has the same powers as a liquidator.

(6) The Central Government may remove a name from the panel on grounds of:
- misconduct;
- fraud;
- misfeasance;
- breach of duties; or
- professional incompetence.

Before doing so, the Central Government must give the person a reasonable opportunity of being heard [verify sub-section numbering].

### Section 276. Removal and replacement of liquidator
(1) For reasonable cause, and for reasons recorded in writing, the Tribunal may remove the provisional liquidator or Company Liquidator on any of these grounds:
- (a) misconduct;
- (b) fraud or misfeasance;
- (c) professional incompetence, or failure to exercise due care and diligence in performing his powers and functions;
- (d) inability to act as provisional liquidator or Company Liquidator; or
- (e) conflict of interest or lack of independence during his term of appointment that would justify removal.

(2) If the provisional liquidator or Company Liquidator dies, resigns or is removed, the Tribunal may, for reasons recorded in writing, transfer his work to another Company Liquidator.

(3) If the Tribunal believes that a provisional liquidator or Company Liquidator caused loss or damage to the company through fraud or misfeasance, or by failing to exercise due care and diligence, it may order recovery of that loss from him. It may also make such other orders as it thinks fit.

(4) The Tribunal must give the liquidator a reasonable opportunity of being heard before passing an order under this section.

### Section 277. Intimation to Company Liquidator, provisional liquidator and Registrar
(1) Within seven days of passing a winding up order, or an order appointing a provisional liquidator, the Tribunal must send notice of the order to the Company Liquidator or provisional liquidator, and to the Registrar.

(2) On receiving a copy of the winding up order, the Registrar must:
- endorse it in his records relating to the company;
- notify the order in the Official Gazette; and
- if the company is listed, also notify the stock exchange(s) where its securities are listed.

(3) A winding up order is treated as notice of discharge to the company's officers, employees and workmen. This does not apply if the company's business is continued.

(4) Within three weeks of the winding up order, the Company Liquidator must apply to the Tribunal to constitute a winding up committee. The committee assists and monitors the progress of liquidation and consists of:
- (a) the Official Liquidator attached to the Tribunal;
- (b) a nominee of the secured creditors; and
- (c) a professional nominated by the Tribunal.

(5) The Company Liquidator convenes the committee's meetings.

(6) The winding up committee assists and monitors the liquidation proceedings in these areas:
- (a) taking over assets;
- (b) examining the statement of affairs;
- (c) recovering property, cash or other assets of the company, including benefits derived from it;
- (d) reviewing audit reports and accounts;
- (e) selling assets;
- (f) finalising the lists of creditors and contributories;
- (g) compromising, abandoning or settling claims;
- (h) paying dividends; and
- (i) any other function the Tribunal may direct.

(7) Every month, the Company Liquidator must place before the Tribunal a report on the committee's work, together with the minutes of its meetings, until the final report for dissolution is submitted.

(8) The Company Liquidator prepares a draft final report for the winding up committee to consider and approve.

(9) The Company Liquidator submits the approved final report to the Tribunal so it can pass a dissolution order.

### Section 278. Effect of winding up order
A winding up order operates in favour of all creditors and all contributories of the company, as if all of them had jointly presented the petition.

### Section 279. Stay of suits, etc., on winding up order
(1) Once a winding up order is made, or a provisional liquidator is appointed, no suit or other legal proceeding may be started by or against the company without the Tribunal's leave. Pending suits or proceedings may not be continued without such leave either. The Tribunal must dispose of an application for leave within sixty days.

(2) Sub-section (1) does not apply to proceedings pending in appeal before the Supreme Court or a High Court.

### Section 280. Jurisdiction of Tribunal
Notwithstanding any other law, the Tribunal has jurisdiction to entertain or dispose of:
- (a) any suit or proceeding by or against the company;
- (b) any claim made by or against the company, including claims by or against any of its branches in India;
- (c) any application made under section 233 [verify: whether the reference is to section 230 or 233]; and
- (d) any question of priorities, or any other question of law or fact, about the company's assets, business, actions, rights, entitlements, privileges, benefits, duties, responsibilities or obligations, or any matter arising out of or relating to its winding up.

This applies whether the suit, proceeding, claim, question or application arose or was instituted before or after the winding up order.

### Section 281. Submission of report by Company Liquidator
(1) Within sixty days of the winding up order, the Company Liquidator must submit a report to the Tribunal containing:
- (a) the nature and details of the company's assets, including their location and value. Cash in hand, cash at bank and negotiable securities are to be shown separately. Assets are to be valued by registered valuers;
- (b) the amount of capital issued, subscribed and paid up;
- (c) the existing and contingent liabilities of the company, including:
  - the names, addresses and occupations of its creditors;
  - secured and unsecured debts, shown separately; and
  - for secured debts, particulars of the securities given, their value and the dates on which they were given;
- (d) the debts due to the company, the names, addresses and occupations of the debtors, and the amount likely to be realised;
- (e) any guarantees given by the company;
- (f) the list of contributories and the dues each of them owes, with details of any unpaid calls;
- (g) details of trade marks and intellectual property, if any, owned by the company;
- (h) details of subsisting contracts, joint ventures and collaborations, if any;
- (i) details of holding and subsidiary companies, if any;
- (j) details of legal cases filed by or against the company; and
- (k) any other information the Tribunal directs or the Company Liquidator considers necessary.

(2) The Company Liquidator must also report:
- how the company was promoted or formed; and
- whether, in his opinion, anyone has committed fraud in the company's promotion or formation, or by any officer in relation to the company since its formation, together with any other matters he thinks fit.

(3) The report must also cover the viability of the company's business, or the steps he considers necessary to maximise the value of the company's assets.

(4) The Company Liquidator may also make further reports to the Tribunal.

(5) Any creditor or contributory, or any other interested person, may inspect the reports under this section and obtain copies on payment of the prescribed fee [verify].

### Section 282. Directions of Tribunal on report of Company Liquidator
(1) After considering the Company Liquidator's report, the Tribunal must fix a time limit for completing the entire proceedings and dissolving the company. The Tribunal may extend or reduce this time limit if it considers proper [verify].

(2) After examining the reports, the Tribunal may order the company to be sold as a going concern, or its assets to be sold in whole or in parts. It may also appoint a sale committee to help the Company Liquidator with the sale. The committee may include creditors, promoters, company officers and others, as the Tribunal decides.

(3) Where the Company Liquidator, the Central Government or any person reports that fraud has been committed in respect of the company, the Tribunal may order an investigation under section 210. This does not affect the winding up proceedings. On considering the investigation report, the Tribunal may pass orders and give directions, including a direction that the person responsible for the fraud be personally liable, or be proceeded against under section 447 [verify].

(4) The Tribunal may order any steps it thinks fit to protect, preserve or enhance the value of the company's assets.

(5) The Tribunal may pass any other order or give any other directions it considers fit.

### Section 283. Custody of company's properties
(1) Once a winding up order is made, or a provisional liquidator is appointed, the Company Liquidator or provisional liquidator must, on the Tribunal's order, immediately take into his custody or control:
- all the company's property and effects;
- its actionable claims; and
- its books and papers [verify].

(2) All property and effects of the company are treated as being in the Tribunal's custody from the date of the winding up order.

(3) On the Tribunal's order, the Company Liquidator may require certain persons to pay, deliver, surrender or transfer to him any money, property or books and papers of the company, immediately or within a time the Tribunal directs. These persons are:
- any contributory;
- any trustee, receiver, banker, agent or officer; or
- any other employee of the company.

### Section 284. Promoters, directors, etc., to cooperate with Company Liquidator
(1) The company's promoters, directors, officers and employees, past and present, must cooperate fully with the Company Liquidator in carrying out his functions and duties.

(2) A person who fails without reasonable cause to meet the obligation in sub-section (1) is punishable with:
- imprisonment up to six months; or
- a fine up to fifty thousand rupees; or
- both [verify; possibly modified by the Companies (Amendment) Act, 2020].

### Section 285. Settlement of list of contributories and application of assets
(1) As soon as may be after the winding up order, the Tribunal must:
- settle a list of contributories;
- have the register of members rectified wherever necessary; and
- have the company's assets applied to discharge its liabilities.

The Tribunal may dispense with settling a list of contributories if it appears that calls on contributories, or adjustment of their rights, will not be needed.

(2) In settling the list, the Tribunal must distinguish between contributories in their own right and contributories who are representatives of others or liable for the debts of others.

(3) The list must include every present or past member who is liable to contribute to the company's assets an amount sufficient to:
- pay its debts and liabilities;
- meet the costs, charges and expenses of winding up; and
- adjust the rights of contributories among themselves.

(4) The liability in sub-section (3) is subject to these limits:
- (a) a past member is not liable if he ceased to be a member one year or more before the winding up began;
- (b) a past member is not liable for any debt or liability contracted after he ceased to be a member;
- (c) a past member is not liable unless the Tribunal finds that the present members cannot make the required contributions;
- (d) for a company limited by shares, no contribution may exceed the amount, if any, unpaid on the shares for which the person is liable as a present or past member;
- (e) for a company limited by guarantee, no contribution may exceed the amount the member undertook to contribute in the event of winding up. If the company also has a share capital, the member is additionally liable up to any amount unpaid on his shares, as if it were a company limited by shares [verify].

### Section 286. Obligations of directors and managers
In the winding up of a limited company, a present or past director or manager whose liability is unlimited under this Act must make a further contribution, beyond his liability as an ordinary member. He contributes as if he had been a member of an unlimited company when the winding up began. This is subject to these limits:
- (a) a past director or manager is not liable if he ceased to hold office one year or more before the winding up began;
- (b) a past director or manager is not liable for any debt or liability contracted after he ceased to hold office;
- (c) subject to the articles, a director or manager is not liable to make the further contribution unless the Tribunal considers it necessary to require it to meet the company's debts, liabilities and the costs of winding up [verify].

### Section 287. Advisory committee
(1) When passing a winding up order, the Tribunal may direct that an advisory committee be set up. The committee advises the Company Liquidator and reports to the Tribunal on the matters the Tribunal directs.

(2) The committee has not more than twelve members. They are creditors and contributories of the company, or other persons, in the proportions the Tribunal directs, having regard to the company's circumstances.

(3) Within thirty days of the winding up order, the Company Liquidator must convene a meeting of the creditors and contributories, as ascertained from the company's books and documents. The purpose is to enable the Tribunal to decide who may be members of the advisory committee.

(4) The advisory committee may inspect the Company Liquidator's books of account and other documents, assets and properties at any reasonable time.

(5) The Company Liquidator chairs the committee's meetings.

(6) Meetings are convened and conducted as prescribed.

(7) The quorum for a meeting is one-third of the members or two members, whichever is higher.

### Section 288. Submission of periodical reports to Tribunal
(1) The Company Liquidator must make periodical reports to the Tribunal, in the prescribed form and manner. In any case, he must report at the end of each quarter on the progress of the winding up.

(2) On an application by the Company Liquidator, the Tribunal may review its orders and make such modifications as it thinks fit.

### Section 289. Power of Tribunal on application for stay of winding up [verify heading]
(1) At any time after making a winding up order, the Tribunal may stay all proceedings relating to the winding up, either completely or for a limited time, on such terms and conditions as it thinks fit. It may do so on an application by the Company Liquidator, a creditor or a contributory, if satisfied that the proceedings ought to be stayed [verify].

(2) Before making the order, the Tribunal may ask the Company Liquidator for a report on any facts or matters relevant to the application [verify].

(3) A copy of every stay order must be forwarded to the Registrar, who must enter it in his records relating to the company [verify].

### Section 290. Powers and duties of Company Liquidator
(1) Subject to the Tribunal's directions, the Company Liquidator in a winding up by the Tribunal has power to:
- (a) carry on the company's business so far as necessary for its beneficial winding up;
- (b) do all acts and execute all deeds, receipts and other documents in the company's name and on its behalf, using the company's seal where necessary;
- (c) sell the company's immovable and movable property and actionable claims, by public auction or private contract. He may transfer the property to a person or body corporate, or sell it in parcels;
- (d) sell the whole undertaking of the company as a going concern;
- (e) raise any money needed, on the security of the company's assets;
- (f) institute or defend any suit, prosecution or other legal proceeding, civil or criminal, in the company's name and on its behalf;
- (g) invite and settle claims of creditors, employees or any other claimant, and distribute sale proceeds according to the priorities established under this Act;
- (h) inspect the company's records and returns on the files of the Registrar or any other authority;
- (i) prove, rank and claim in the insolvency of any contributory for any balance against his estate, and receive dividends in that insolvency for that balance, as a separate debt due from the insolvent, rateably with other separate creditors;
- (j) draw, accept, make and endorse any negotiable instrument, including a cheque, bill of exchange, hundi or promissory note, in the company's name and on its behalf. This has the same effect on the company's liability as if the company had done it in the course of its business;
- (k) take out letters of administration to a deceased contributory in his official name, and do any other act needed to obtain payment of money due from a contributory or his estate that cannot conveniently be done in the company's name;
- (l) obtain professional assistance from any person, or appoint any professional, to discharge his duties, obligations and responsibilities and to protect the company's assets. He may also appoint an agent to do any business he cannot do himself;
- (m) take any action or step, or sign, execute and verify any paper, deed, document, application, petition, affidavit, bond or instrument, as necessary for:
  - (i) winding up the company;
  - (ii) distributing its assets; and
  - (iii) discharging his duties, obligations and functions as Company Liquidator; and
- (n) apply to the Tribunal for any orders or directions needed for the winding up.

(2) The Company Liquidator exercises these powers subject to the overall control of the Tribunal.

(3) Notwithstanding sub-section (1), the Company Liquidator must also perform any other duties the Tribunal specifies.

### Section 291. Provision for professional assistance to Company Liquidator
(1) With the Tribunal's sanction, the Company Liquidator may appoint one or more of the following to assist him in performing his duties and functions, on such terms and conditions as necessary:
- chartered accountants;
- company secretaries;
- cost accountants;
- legal practitioners; or
- other professionals.

(2) A person so appointed must immediately disclose to the Tribunal, in the prescribed form, any conflict of interest or lack of independence regarding his appointment.

### Section 292. Exercise and control of Company Liquidator's powers
(1) In administering the company's assets and distributing them among creditors, the Company Liquidator must have regard to any directions given by:
- a resolution of the creditors or contributories at a general meeting; or
- the advisory committee.

(2) If directions conflict, directions of the creditors or contributories at a general meeting override directions of the advisory committee.

(3) The Company Liquidator may summon general meetings of creditors or contributories to ascertain their wishes. He must summon such meetings when:
- directed to do so by a resolution of creditors or contributories; or
- requested in writing by at least one-tenth in value of the creditors or contributories [verify].

(4) The Company Liquidator may apply to the Tribunal for directions on any particular matter arising in the winding up.

(5) Subject to this Chapter, the Company Liquidator uses his own discretion in managing the estate and distributing the assets.

(6) Any person aggrieved by an act or decision of the Company Liquidator may apply to the Tribunal. The Tribunal may confirm, reverse or modify the act or decision, and make such further order as it thinks just.

### Section 293. Books to be kept by Company Liquidator
The Company Liquidator must keep proper books, in the prescribed manner, recording entries or minutes of proceedings at meetings and other prescribed matters. Subject to the Tribunal's control, any creditor or contributory may inspect these books, personally or through an agent.

### Section 294. Audit of Company Liquidator's accounts
(1) The Company Liquidator must maintain proper and regular books of account, in the prescribed manner, including accounts of receipts and payments.

(2) At the prescribed times, and at least twice in each year of his tenure, he must present to the Tribunal an account of his receipts and payments as liquidator. The account must be:
- in the prescribed form;
- in duplicate; and
- verified by a declaration in the prescribed form.

(3) The Tribunal has the accounts audited in whatever manner it thinks fit. For the audit, the Company Liquidator must provide the Tribunal with the necessary vouchers and information. The Tribunal may require production of, and inspect, any books of account he keeps.

(4) Once audited, one copy of the account is filed with the Tribunal and the other is delivered to the Registrar. Each copy is open to inspection by any creditor, contributory or interested person.

(5) Where the account relates to a Government company in liquidation, the Company Liquidator must send a copy to:
- the Central Government, if it is a member of the company;
- the State Government, if it is a member; or
- both, if both are members.

### Section 295. Payment of debts by contributory and extent of set-off
(1) At any time after the winding up order, the Tribunal may order any contributory on the list to pay, as directed, any money he (or the estate of the person he represents) owes the company. This excludes money payable by virtue of any call made under this Act.

(2) When making such an order, the Tribunal may allow set-off as follows:
- (a) for an unlimited company, the contributory may set off money due to him or his estate from the company on any independent dealing or contract. This does not cover money due to him as a member by way of dividend or profit;
- (b) for a limited company, a director or manager whose liability is unlimited, or his estate, may be given a similar allowance.

(3) For any company, limited or unlimited, once all creditors have been paid in full, any money due from the company to a contributory on any account may be set off against any later call.

### Section 296. Power of Tribunal to make calls
(1) At any time after the winding up order, and whether before or after it has ascertained whether the company's assets are sufficient, the Tribunal may:
- (a) make calls on all or any contributories on the list, to the extent of their liability. The purpose is to pay any money the Tribunal considers necessary to:
  - satisfy the company's debts and liabilities;
  - meet the costs, charges and expenses of winding up; and
  - adjust the rights of contributories among themselves; and
- (b) order payment of any calls so made.

(2) In making a call, the Tribunal may take into account the probability that some contributories may fail to pay all or part of it.

### Section 297. Adjustment of rights of contributories
The Tribunal adjusts the rights of contributories among themselves and distributes any surplus among those entitled to it.

### Section 298. Power to order costs
If the company's assets are insufficient to meet its liabilities, the Tribunal may order the costs, charges and expenses of the winding up to be paid out of the assets. They are paid in whatever order of priority the Tribunal thinks fit.

### Section 299. Power to summon persons suspected of having property of company, etc.
(1) At any time after appointing a provisional liquidator or making a winding up order, the Tribunal may summon any of the following persons to appear before it or before the Company Liquidator:
- any officer of the company;
- any person known or suspected to possess any property or books or papers of the company;
- any person known or suspected to owe money to the company; or
- any person the Tribunal considers able to give information about the company's promotion, formation, trade, dealings, property, books or papers, or affairs.

(2) The Tribunal may examine such a person on oath, orally or by written interrogatories. It may reduce his answers to writing and require him to sign them.

(3) The Tribunal may require such a person to produce any books and papers relating to the company that are in his custody or power. If he claims a lien over the books or papers, producing them does not prejudice the lien. The Tribunal has jurisdiction in the winding up to decide all questions about the lien.

(4) If a person so summoned is tendered a reasonable sum for expenses and fails, without reasonable excuse, to appear at the appointed time, the Tribunal may impose punishment [verify]. The provision is understood to provide for a fine up to one lakh rupees, and possibly also for having him apprehended and produced for examination [verify].

### Section 300. Power to order examination of promoters, directors, etc.
(1) This section applies where:
- the Tribunal has ordered a company to be wound up; and
- the Company Liquidator has reported under section 281 that, in his opinion, a fraud has been committed by any person in the promotion, formation, business or conduct of the company's affairs since its formation.

After considering the report, the Tribunal may direct that person or officer to attend before it on an appointed day. He is then publicly examined about the promotion or formation or the conduct of the company's business, or his conduct and dealings as an officer.

(2) The Company Liquidator takes part in the examination. If authorised by the Tribunal, he may employ legal assistance for this purpose.

(3) Any creditor or contributory may also take part, personally or through a chartered accountant, company secretary, cost accountant or legal practitioner entitled to appear before the Tribunal under section 432.

(4) The Tribunal may put any questions to the person being examined that it thinks fit.

(5) The person is examined on oath and must answer every question the Tribunal puts or allows to be put to him.

(6) At his own cost, the person ordered to be examined may employ a chartered accountant, company secretary, cost accountant or legal practitioner entitled to appear before the Tribunal. That professional may put questions the Tribunal considers just, to help the person explain or qualify any answer.

(7) Notes of the examination are taken down in writing, read over or given to the person, and signed by him. They may be used in evidence against him and are open to inspection by any creditor or contributory at all reasonable times.

(8) Where the Tribunal concludes from the examination (whether or not a criminal prosecution has been instituted) that the person has committed fraud, it may direct the Company Liquidator to prosecute him or to refer the matter to the Registrar, or pass other orders [verify].

### Section 301. Arrest of person trying to leave India or abscond
At any time before or after making a winding up order, the Tribunal may act under this section if there is probable cause to believe that a contributory, or a director, manager or other officer of the company, is about to:
- leave India;
- otherwise abscond; or
- remove or conceal any of his property,

in order to evade payment of calls or avoid examination about the company's affairs.

In that case, the Tribunal may have:
- (a) the contributory, director, manager or officer detained until such time as it orders; and
- (b) his books and papers and movable property seized and safely kept until such time as it orders.

### Section 302. Dissolution of company by Tribunal
(1) When the company's affairs have been completely wound up, the Company Liquidator must apply to the Tribunal for dissolution of the company.

(2) On that application, or whenever it considers it just and reasonable, the Tribunal must order that the company be dissolved from the date of the order. The company is dissolved accordingly.

(3) Within thirty days of the order, the Company Liquidator must forward a copy of it to the Registrar. The Registrar records a minute of the dissolution in the register relating to the company.

(4) If the Company Liquidator fails to forward the copy within thirty days, he is punishable with a fine up to five thousand rupees for every day the default continues [verify: may have been converted to a monetary penalty by the Companies (Amendment) Act, 2020].

### Section 303. Appeals from orders [verify heading and text]
Any person aggrieved by an order or decision of the Tribunal in a winding up under this Part may appeal to the Appellate Tribunal. The appeal must be filed within the time and in the manner provided under this Act [verify: the substantive text of this section is not reliably recalled; appeals generally lie under section 421].

### PART III — PROVISIONS APPLICABLE TO EVERY MODE OF WINDING UP

### Section 324. Debts provable
In every winding up, the following are admissible to proof against the company:
- debts payable on a contingency; and
- all claims against the company, whether present or future, certain or contingent, ascertained, or sounding only in damages.

A just estimate is to be made, as far as possible, of the value of debts or claims that:
- are subject to a contingency;
- sound only in damages; or
- for any other reason do not bear a certain value.

### Section 325. Application of insolvency rules in winding up of insolvent companies
(1) In the winding up of an insolvent company, the rules in force under the law of insolvency for the estates of persons adjudged insolvent apply to:
- (a) debts provable;
- (b) the valuation of annuities and of future and contingent liabilities; and
- (c) the respective rights of secured and unsecured creditors.

Proviso: Every secured creditor's security is treated as subject to a pari passu charge in favour of the workmen, to the extent of the workmen's portion in it. If a secured creditor chooses to realise his security instead of relinquishing it and proving his debt:
- (a) the liquidator may represent the workmen and enforce that charge;
- (b) any amount the liquidator realises by enforcing the charge is applied rateably to discharge the workmen's dues; and
- (c) the lesser of the following ranks pari passu with the workmen's dues for the purposes of section 326:
  - the part of the secured creditor's debt that he could not realise; or
  - the amount of the workmen's portion in his security.

(2) Everyone who would be entitled in such a case to prove for and receive dividends out of the company's assets may come in under the winding up and make their claims.

Explanation: In this section, "workmen", "workmen's dues" and "workmen's portion" have the meanings given in the Explanation to section 326 [verify].

### Section 326. Overriding preferential payments
(1) In the winding up of a company under this Act, the following debts are paid in priority to all other debts:
- (a) workmen's dues; and
- (b) where a secured creditor has realised a secured asset, the lesser of:
  - the part of his debt he could not realise; or
  - the amount of the workmen's portion in his security (if payable under law).

The amount under (b) ranks pari passu with workmen's dues.

Proviso: In the winding up of a company, the sums in sub-clauses (i) and (ii) of clause (b) of the Explanation (wages and salary, and accrued holiday remuneration) are given special treatment. To the extent they are payable for the two years before the winding up order, or any other prescribed period:
- they are paid in priority to all other debts, including debts due to secured creditors;
- they must be paid within thirty days of the sale of assets; and
- they are subject to such charge over the security as may be prescribed.

(2) The debts under the proviso to sub-section (1) are paid in full before any payment to secured creditors. After that, the debts under sub-section (1) are paid in full. If the assets are insufficient, those debts abate in equal proportions.

Explanation: In this section:
- (a) "workmen", in relation to a company, means the employees of the company who are workmen within the meaning of clause (s) of section 2 of the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947;
- (b) "workmen's dues", in relation to a company, means the total of the following amounts owed by the company to its workmen:
  - (i) all wages or salary, including wages for time or piece work, and salary earned wholly or partly by commission, for services rendered to the company. This includes any compensation payable to a workman under any provision of the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947;
  - (ii) all accrued holiday remuneration that becomes payable to a workman (or, if he has died, to any other person in his right) when his employment ends before, or because of, the winding up order or resolution;
  - (iii) all amounts due as compensation, or liability for compensation, under the Workmen's Compensation Act, 1923 for the death or disablement of any workman of the company. This does not apply if:
    - the company is being wound up voluntarily only for reconstruction or amalgamation with another company; or
    - when the winding up began, the company had rights under a contract with insurers of the kind mentioned in section 14 of that Act that could be transferred to and vested in the workman;
  - (iv) all sums due to any workman from a provident fund, pension fund, gratuity fund or any other fund for the welfare of workmen maintained by the company;
- (c) "workmen's portion", in relation to the security of a secured creditor, means the amount that bears the same proportion to the value of the security as the workmen's dues bear to the total of the workmen's dues and the debts due to secured creditors.

Illustration: The value of the security of a secured creditor is Rs. 1,00,000. The total workmen's dues are Rs. 1,00,000 and the debts due to secured creditors are Rs. 3,00,000. The total of the two is Rs. 4,00,000. The workmen's portion is therefore one-fourth of the value of the security, that is, Rs. 25,000.

### Section 327. Preferential payments
(1) In the winding up of a company under this Act, the following are paid in priority to all other debts, subject to section 326:
- (a) all revenues, taxes, cesses and rates due from the company to the Central Government, a State Government or a local authority on the relevant date, which became due and payable within the twelve months immediately before that date;
- (b) all wages or salary of any employee for services rendered to the company, due for a period of up to four months within the twelve months immediately before the relevant date. This includes wages for time or piece work and salary earned wholly or partly by commission. The amount payable under this clause to any one workman may not exceed such amount as may be notified;
- (c) all accrued holiday remuneration that becomes payable to any employee (or, if he has died, to any person claiming under him) when his employment ends before, or because of, the winding up order or dissolution of the company;
- (d) all amounts due as contributions payable by the company as employer during the twelve months immediately before the relevant date, under the Employees' State Insurance Act, 1948 or any other law. This does not apply where the company is being wound up voluntarily only for reconstruction or amalgamation with another company;
- (e) all amounts due as compensation, or liability for compensation, under the Workmen's Compensation Act, 1923 for the death or disablement of any employee of the company. This does not apply where, when the winding up began, the company had rights under an insurance contract of the kind mentioned in section 14 of that Act that could be transferred to and vested in the workman;
- (f) all sums due to any employee from a provident fund, pension fund, gratuity fund or any other fund for the welfare of employees maintained by the company; and
- (g) the expenses of any investigation held under section 213 or section 216, so far as they are payable by the company.

(2) Where money advanced by any person was used to pay an employee wages, salary or accrued holiday remuneration, that person has a right of priority in the winding up. The priority is in respect of the money advanced and paid, up to the amount by which the employee's own priority has been reduced by that payment.

(3) The debts under sub-section (1):
- (a) rank equally among themselves and are paid in full. If the assets are insufficient, they abate in equal proportions; and
- (b) to the extent the assets available to general creditors are insufficient to meet them, have priority over the claims of debenture holders under any floating charge created by the company. They are paid accordingly out of any property comprised in or subject to that charge.

(4) The debts under sub-section (1) must be discharged immediately, so far as the assets are sufficient, after retaining the sums needed for the costs and expenses of the winding up.

(5) If a landlord or other person has distrained on the company's goods or effects within the three months immediately before the winding up order, the debts given priority under this section are a first charge on those goods or effects, or on the proceeds of their sale. That person then has the same rights of priority as the persons to whom payment is made.

(6) Debts under this section are payable in addition to those under section 326.

(7) Sections 326 and 327 do not apply in a liquidation under the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 2016. (Inserted by the IBC, 2016.)

Explanation: For the purposes of this section:
- (a) remuneration for a period of holiday, or of absence from work through sickness or other good cause, is treated as wages for services rendered during that period;
- (b) "accrued holiday remuneration" includes any sum that would have become payable to the employee as holiday remuneration if his employment had continued until he became entitled to a holiday, under an order or direction made under any law or any contract;
- (c) "employee" does not include a workman; and
- (d) "relevant date" means, for a company being wound up by the Tribunal, the date of appointment (or first appointment) of a provisional liquidator. If no provisional liquidator was appointed, it is the date of the winding up order, unless the company had commenced to be wound up voluntarily before that date [verify].

### Section 328. Fraudulent preference
(1) This sub-section applies where a company has given a preference to a creditor, or to a surety or guarantor for any of its debts or liabilities. The preference consists of the company doing, or allowing to be done, anything that puts that person in a better position in a liquidation than he would otherwise have had. If this happened within six months before the winding up application, and the Tribunal is satisfied it was a fraudulent preference, the Tribunal may order whatever it thinks fit to restore the position to what it would have been without the preference.

(2) This sub-section applies where, within six months before the winding up application, there has been, by or against a company, a preferential:
- transfer of movable or immovable property;
- delivery of goods;
- payment; or
- execution made, taken or done.

If the Tribunal is satisfied of this, it may order as it thinks fit, declare the transaction invalid, and restore the position.

### Section 329. Transfers not in good faith to be void
A transfer of movable or immovable property, or a delivery of goods, by a company is void against the Company Liquidator if all of the following apply:
- it was not made in the ordinary course of the company's business;
- it was not made to a purchaser or encumbrancer in good faith and for valuable consideration; and
- it was made within one year before a petition for winding up by the Tribunal under this Act was presented.

(References to voluntary winding up resolutions were removed by the IBC, 2016 [verify].)

### Section 330. Certain transfers to be void
Any transfer or assignment by a company of all its property or assets to trustees for the benefit of all its creditors is void.

### Section 331. Liabilities and rights of certain persons fraudulently preferred
(1) This sub-section applies where anything made or done is void under section 328 as a fraudulent preference of a person interested in property that is mortgaged or charged to secure the company's debt. In that case, the preferred person has the same liabilities and rights as if he had undertaken to be personally liable as surety for the debt. His liability is limited to the lesser of:
- the amount of the mortgage or charge on the property; or
- the value of his interest.

This does not affect any rights or liabilities arising apart from this provision.

(2) The value of the preferred person's interest is determined as at the date of the transaction constituting the fraudulent preference. It is calculated as if the interest were free of all encumbrances other than those to which the mortgage or charge for the company's debt was then subject.

(3) This sub-section applies to an application to the Tribunal about a payment alleged to be a fraudulent preference of a surety or guarantor. The Tribunal may decide any question about the payment arising between the payee and the surety or guarantor, and grant relief, even if this is not needed for the winding up. For that purpose, it may allow the surety or guarantor to be brought in as a third party, as in a suit to recover the sum paid.

(4) Sub-section (3) applies, with necessary changes, to transactions other than the payment of money.

### Section 332. Effect of floating charge
Where a company is being wound up, a floating charge on its undertaking or property created within the twelve months immediately before the winding up began is invalid, unless it is proved that the company was solvent immediately after the charge was created. The charge remains valid only for:
- the amount of any cash paid to the company at the time of, or after, the creation of the charge, in consideration for the charge; and
- interest on that amount at five per cent per annum, or any other rate the Central Government notifies.

### Section 333. Disclaimer of onerous property
(1) This sub-section applies where any part of the property of a company being wound up consists of:
- (a) land of any tenure that is burdened with onerous covenants;
- (b) shares or stocks in companies;
- (c) any other property that is unsaleable, or not readily saleable, because its owner is bound to perform an onerous act or pay a sum of money; or
- (d) unprofitable contracts.

With the Tribunal's leave, the Company Liquidator may disclaim the property by a signed writing. This applies even if he has tried to sell it, taken possession of it, exercised acts of ownership over it, or done anything under the contract. The disclaimer must be made within twelve months after the winding up began, or within any longer period the Tribunal allows.

Proviso: If the Company Liquidator was not aware of the property within one month after the winding up began, he may disclaim it within twelve months after he becomes aware of it, or within any longer period the Tribunal allows.

(2) A disclaimer ends, from its date, the rights, interests and liabilities of the company and its property in or regarding the disclaimed property. It does not affect anyone else's rights or liabilities, except so far as necessary to release the company and its property from liability.

(3) Before or when granting leave to disclaim, the Tribunal may:
- require notices to be given to interested persons;
- impose terms as a condition of granting leave; and
- make any other order it considers just.

(4) The Company Liquidator loses the right to disclaim if:
- any interested person has applied to him in writing asking whether he will disclaim; and
- within twenty-eight days of receiving the application, or any longer period the Tribunal allows, he has not given notice that he intends to apply to the Tribunal for leave to disclaim.

For a contract, if he does not disclaim within that period after such an application, he is treated as having adopted the contract.

(5) A person who, as against the Company Liquidator, is entitled to the benefit or subject to the burden of a contract with the company may apply to the Tribunal. The Tribunal may rescind the contract on terms it considers just, including payment of damages for non-performance by or to either party. Damages payable to that person may be proved by him as a debt in the winding up.

(6) A person may apply to the Tribunal if he claims an interest in disclaimed property, or is under a liability regarding it that this Act has not discharged. After hearing such persons as it thinks fit, the Tribunal may order the property to be vested in or delivered to:
- persons entitled to it;
- persons to whom it seems just to deliver it as compensation for such a liability; or
- a trustee for such a person,

on terms the Tribunal considers just.

(7) A person injured by a disclaimer under this section is treated as a creditor of the company for the amount of the injury, and may prove that amount as a debt in the winding up.

### Section 334. Transfers, etc., after commencement of winding up to be void
(1) In a winding up by the Tribunal, the following are void unless the Tribunal orders otherwise, if made after the winding up began:
- any disposition of the company's property, including actionable claims;
- any transfer of shares in the company; and
- any change in the status of its members.

(2) [verify: the sub-section on transfers of shares in a voluntary winding up without the liquidator's sanction was omitted or amended by the IBC, 2016.]

### Section 335. Certain attachments, executions, etc., in winding up by Tribunal to be void
(1) Where a company is being wound up by the Tribunal, the following are void:
- (a) any attachment, distress or execution put in force against the company's estate or effects after the winding up began, without the Tribunal's leave; and
- (b) any sale of the company's properties or effects held after the winding up began, without the Tribunal's leave.

(2) This section does not apply to proceedings to recover any tax, impost or other dues payable to the Government.

### Section 336. Offences by officers of companies in liquidation
(1) This sub-section applies to any person who is or has been an officer of a company that, at the time of the alleged offence, is being wound up by the Tribunal under this Act, or is later ordered to be so wound up. Such a person commits an offence if he:
- (a) does not, to the best of his knowledge and belief, fully and truly disclose to the Company Liquidator:
  - all the company's movable and immovable property; and
  - how, to whom, for what consideration and when the company disposed of any part of it,

  except property disposed of in the ordinary course of business;
- (b) does not deliver to the Company Liquidator, or as he directs, all of the company's movable and immovable property in his custody or control that he is legally required to deliver;
- (c) does not deliver to the Company Liquidator, or as he directs, all books of account and papers of the company in his custody or control that he is legally required to deliver;
- (d) within the twelve months before the winding up began, or at any time afterwards:
  - (i) conceals any part of the company's property worth one thousand rupees or more, or conceals any debt due to or from the company;
  - (ii) fraudulently removes any part of the company's property worth one thousand rupees or more;
  - (iii) conceals, destroys, mutilates or falsifies any book or paper affecting or relating to the company's property or affairs, or is privy to this;
  - (iv) makes a false entry in any such book or paper, or is privy to this;
  - (v) fraudulently parts with, alters or omits anything from any such book or paper, or is privy to this;
  - (vi) by a false representation or other fraud, obtains property on credit for or on behalf of the company, which the company does not later pay for;
  - (vii) under the false pretence that the company is carrying on business, obtains property on credit for or on behalf of the company, which the company does not later pay for; or
  - (viii) pawns, pledges or disposes of company property that was obtained on credit and not paid for, unless this is done in the ordinary course of the company's business;
- (e) makes any material omission in a statement relating to the company's affairs;
- (f) knows or believes that a false debt has been proved by any person in the winding up, and fails to inform the Company Liquidator for one month;
- (g) after the winding up begins, prevents the production of any book or paper affecting or relating to the company's property or affairs;
- (h) after the winding up begins, or at any meeting of creditors within the twelve months before it began, attempts to account for any part of the company's property by fictitious losses or expenses; or
- (i) within the twelve months before the winding up began, or at any time afterwards, obtains by a false representation or other fraud the consent of the company's creditors (or any of them) to an agreement about the company's affairs or the winding up [verify: there may be further clauses].

Punishment: imprisonment of not less than three years, which may extend to five years, and a fine of not less than one lakh rupees, which may extend to three lakh rupees.
