- the foreign company is liable to a penalty of one lakh rupees, plus five hundred rupees for each day after the first that the contravention continues, subject to a maximum of three lakh rupees; and
- every officer in default is liable to a penalty of twenty-five thousand rupees, plus one hundred rupees for each day after the first that the contravention continues, subject to a maximum of one lakh rupees.

(Original 2013 text: the foreign company was punishable with a fine of not less than one lakh rupees, extending to three lakh rupees, and a further fine up to fifty thousand rupees per day for a continuing offence. Every officer in default was punishable with imprisonment up to six months, or a fine of twenty-five thousand rupees to five lakh rupees, or both [verify].)

### Section 393. Company's failure to comply with provisions of this Chapter not to affect validity of contracts, etc.
A company's failure to comply with this Chapter does not affect the validity of any contract, dealing or transaction it has entered into, or its liability to be sued on it. However, until the company has complied with this Chapter, it may not do any of the following in respect of any such contract, dealing or transaction:
- bring a suit;
- claim a set-off;
- make a counter-claim; or
- institute any legal proceeding.

### Section 393A. Exemptions
(Inserted by the Companies (Amendment) Act, 2020 [verify].) The Central Government may exempt any class of foreign companies, or companies incorporated or to be incorporated outside India, from any of the provisions of this Chapter. It must do so by notification [verify]. A copy of every such notification must be laid before each House of Parliament as soon as may be after it is issued.

(Under this power, notifications have been issued, for example for companies incorporated in permissible jurisdictions with respect to listing of securities on stock exchanges in IFSCs [verify].)

## CHAPTER XXIII — GOVERNMENT COMPANIES

### Section 394. Annual reports on Government companies
(1) Where the Central Government is a member of a Government company, it must cause an annual report on the company's working and affairs to be:
- (a) prepared within three months of the annual general meeting before which the audit report is placed; and
- (b) laid before both Houses of Parliament as soon as may be after preparation, together with:
  - a copy of the audit report; and
  - any comments on, or supplement to, the audit report made by the Comptroller and Auditor-General of India.

(2) Where a State Government is also a member of the Government company in addition to the Central Government, the State Government must cause a copy of the annual report under sub-section (1) to be laid before the House or both Houses of the State Legislature. The audit report and the comments on or supplement to it must be laid with the report.

### Section 395. Annual reports where one or more State Governments are members of companies
(1) Where the Central Government is not a member of a Government company, every State Government that is a member (or the only State Government that is a member) must cause an annual report on the company's working and affairs to be:
- (a) prepared within three months of the annual general meeting before which the audit report is placed; and
- (b) laid before the House or both Houses of the State Legislature as soon as may be after preparation, together with:
  - a copy of the audit report; and
  - the comments on, or supplement to, the audit report made by the Comptroller and Auditor-General of India.

(2) This section and section 394 apply, as far as may be, to a Government company in liquidation as they apply to any other Government company.

## CHAPTER XXIV — REGISTRATION OFFICES AND FEES

### Section 396. Registration offices
(1) For registration of companies and discharge of functions under this Act, the Central Government must, by notification, establish such number of offices at such places as it thinks fit, specifying their jurisdiction. (The Central Registration Centre and the Centre for Processing Accelerated Corporate Exit (C-PACE) have been established by notifications under this section [verify].)

(2) The Central Government may appoint such Registrars, Additional, Joint, Deputy and Assistant Registrars as it considers necessary for registration of companies and discharge of functions under this Act. Their powers and duties are as prescribed.

(3) The terms and conditions of service, including salaries, of persons appointed under sub-section (2) are as prescribed.

(4) The Central Government may direct that a seal or seals be prepared for authenticating documents required for, or connected with, the registration of companies.

### Section 397. Admissibility of certain documents as evidence
Notwithstanding any other law, the following are treated as documents for the purposes of this Act and the rules, and are admissible in any proceedings without further proof or production of the original, as evidence of the contents of the original or of any fact stated in it of which direct evidence would be admissible:
- any document reproduced from, or as a copy or printout of, any document submitted, filed, registered or recorded in electronic form under this Act; and
- any computer printout or electronic record of such a document,

provided the prescribed conditions, if any, are met [verify].

### Section 398. Provisions relating to filing of applications, documents, inspection, etc., in electronic form
(1) Notwithstanding anything to the contrary in this Act, and without prejudice to section 6 of the Information Technology Act, 2000, the Central Government may make rules requiring, from a prescribed date, that:
- (a) applications, balance sheets, prospectuses, returns, declarations, memoranda, articles, particulars of charges, and other particulars or documents required to be filed or delivered under this Act or the rules are filed in electronic form and authenticated as prescribed;
- (b) documents, notices, communications or intimations required to be served or delivered under this Act are served or delivered in electronic form and authenticated as prescribed;
- (c) applications, balance sheets, prospectuses, returns, registers, memoranda, articles, particulars of charges, and other particulars or documents filed under this Act or the rules are maintained by the Registrar in electronic form and registered or authenticated as prescribed;
- (d) documents maintained in electronic form that are otherwise available for inspection under this Act or the rules may be inspected by any person electronically, as prescribed. This covers memoranda, articles, registers, indexes, balance sheets, returns, and other particulars or documents;
- (e) fees, charges and other sums payable under this Act or the rules are paid electronically, as prescribed; and
- (f) the Registrar performs his functions in electronic form, as prescribed. These include registering changes of registered office, alterations of memorandum or articles, and prospectuses; issuing certificates of incorporation; registering documents; issuing certificates; recording notices; receiving communications; and performing any other act this Act directs him to do.

Explanation: Rules under this section do not relate to:
- the imposition of fines or other pecuniary penalties;
- the demand or payment of fees; or
- contraventions of this Act or punishment for them.

(2) Electronic forms are as prescribed. Documents filed electronically are treated as filed in compliance with this Act [verify].

(3) The Information Technology Act, 2000 applies to records in electronic form under clauses (a) to (f) of sub-section (1).

### Section 399. Inspection, production and evidence of documents kept by Registrar
(1) Except as otherwise provided in this Act, any person may:
- (a) inspect by electronic means any documents kept by the Registrar under rules made under the Companies Act, 1956 or this Act, on payment of the prescribed fee for each inspection. This covers:
  - documents filed or registered under this Act; and
  - records of any fact required or authorised to be recorded or registered under this Act;
- (b) on payment in advance of the prescribed fee, require:
  - a certificate of incorporation of any company; or
  - a copy or extract of any other document, or part of a document, certified by the Registrar.

These rights are exercisable as follows:
- (i) for documents delivered to the Registrar with a prospectus under section 26, only during the fourteen days beginning with the date of publication of the prospectus, and at other times only with the Central Government's permission;
- (ii) for documents delivered under section 388(1)(b), only during the fourteen days beginning with the date of the prospectus, and at other times only with the Central Government's permission.

(2) No court or Tribunal may issue process compelling production of any document kept by the Registrar except with its leave. Any such process must state that it is issued with that leave.

(3) A copy of, or extract from, any document kept and registered at an office for registration of companies, certified as a true copy by the Registrar, is admissible in evidence in all legal proceedings as equally valid as the original. The Registrar's official position need not be proved.

### Section 400. Electronic form to be exclusive, alternative or in addition to physical form
The Central Government may, by notification, provide that such documents and procedures under this Act as may be specified must be filed, served, maintained or complied with in electronic form, either:
- exclusively;
- as an alternative to physical form; or
- in addition to physical form [verify].

### Section 401. Provision of value added services through electronic form
The Central Government may provide such value added services through electronic form, and levy such fees for them, as may be prescribed.

### Section 402. Application of provisions of Information Technology Act, 2000
So far as they are not inconsistent with this Act, all provisions of the Information Technology Act, 2000 on electronic records apply to records in electronic form under this Act. This includes the manner and format in which electronic records are to be filed.

### Section 403. Fee for filing, etc.
(1) Any document required to be submitted, filed, registered or recorded, or any fact or information required or authorised to be registered, under this Act must be submitted, filed, registered or recorded within the time specified in the relevant provision, on payment of the prescribed fee.

Provisos (as substituted by the Companies (Amendment) Act, 2017 and amended in 2019 [verify]):
- The document, fact or information may be submitted, filed, registered or recorded after the period provided in the relevant section, on payment of the prescribed additional fee. (The original 2013 text allowed this only within two hundred and seventy days, on payment of additional fee.)
- Where it is not done within the period provided in the relevant section, a higher additional fee as prescribed is levied, without prejudice to any other legal action or liability under this Act. This higher fee may not be less than twice the additional fee under the first proviso [verify].

(2) If a company fails to submit, file, register or record any document, fact or information under sub-section (1) before the period specified in the relevant section expires, the company and its officers in default are liable to the penalty or punishment provided under this Act for that failure. This is in addition to their liability to pay the fee and additional fee.

### Section 404. Fees, etc., to be credited into public account
All fees, charges and other sums received under this Act by any of the following must be paid into the public account of India:
- a Registrar;
- an Additional, Joint, Deputy or Assistant Registrar; or
- any other officer of the Central Government.

## CHAPTER XXV — COMPANIES TO FURNISH INFORMATION OR STATISTICS

### Section 405. Power of Central Government to direct companies to furnish information or statistics
(1) The Central Government may, by order, require companies generally, any class of companies, or any company to furnish information or statistics about their constitution or working, within the time specified in the order.

(2) An order under sub-section (1) addressed to companies generally, or to a class of companies, must be published in the Official Gazette. It may be served on a company in any manner the Central Government thinks fit. The date of publication or service is treated as the date on which the information or statistics were requisitioned.

(3) To satisfy itself that the information or statistics furnished are correct and complete, the Central Government may by order require the company:
- (a) to produce records or documents in its possession, or allow an officer it specifies to inspect them; or
- (b) to furnish further information,

within the time specified in the order.

(4) The following applies if a company fails to comply with an order under sub-section (1) or (3), or knowingly furnishes information or statistics that are incorrect or incomplete in any material respect (as substituted by the Companies (Amendment) Act, 2020 [verify all amounts]):
- the company is liable to a penalty of twenty thousand rupees, plus one thousand rupees for each day after the first that the failure continues, subject to a maximum of three lakh rupees; and
- every officer in default is liable to a penalty of twenty-five thousand rupees [verify]. Imprisonment may also apply where false information is knowingly furnished [verify].

(Original 2013 text: the company was punishable with a fine up to twenty-five thousand rupees. Every officer in default was punishable with imprisonment up to six months, or a fine of twenty-five thousand rupees to three lakh rupees, or both [verify].)

(5) The Central Government may, by notification, direct that the provisions of this section apply also to bodies corporate other than companies, or to any class of them [verify].

## CHAPTER XXVI — NIDHIS

### Section 406. Power to modify Act in its application to Nidhis
(1) In this section, "Nidhi" or "Mutual Benefit Society" means a company that the Central Government declares, by notification in the Official Gazette, to be a Nidhi or Mutual Benefit Society, as the case may be.

(As amended by the Companies (Amendment) Act, 2019 [verify]. Following the 2019 amendment and the Nidhi (Amendment) Rules, 2022, a company intending to function as a Nidhi must apply to the Central Government for declaration as a Nidhi [verify].)

(2) The Central Government may, by notification, direct that any provision of this Act specified in the notification:
- (a) does not apply to any Nidhi or class of Nidhis; or
- (b) applies to any Nidhi or class of Nidhis with the exceptions, modifications and adaptations specified in the notification.

(3) A copy of every notification proposed under sub-section (2) must be laid in draft before each House of Parliament, while it is in session, for a total of thirty days. The thirty days may fall in one session or in two or more successive sessions. If, before the end of the session immediately following, both Houses agree to disapprove the notification, it must not be issued. If both Houses agree to modify it, it may be issued only in that modified form.

## CHAPTER XXVII — NATIONAL COMPANY LAW TRIBUNAL AND APPELLATE TRIBUNAL

### Section 407. Definitions
In this Chapter, unless the context requires otherwise:
- (a) "Chairperson" means the Chairperson of the Appellate Tribunal;
- (b) "Judicial Member" means a member of the Tribunal or Appellate Tribunal appointed as such, and includes the President or the Chairperson, as the case may be;
- (c) "Member" means a member, Judicial or Technical, of the Tribunal or Appellate Tribunal, and includes the President or the Chairperson, as the case may be;
- (d) "President" means the President of the Tribunal; and
- (e) "Technical Member" means a member of the Tribunal or Appellate Tribunal appointed as such.

### Section 408. Constitution of National Company Law Tribunal
The Central Government must, by notification, constitute a Tribunal called the National Company Law Tribunal, with effect from the date specified in the notification. The Tribunal consists of:
- a President; and
- such number of Judicial and Technical Members as the Central Government considers necessary, appointed by it by notification.

The Tribunal exercises and discharges the powers and functions conferred on it by or under this Act or any other law. (The NCLT was constituted with effect from 1-6-2016 [verify].)

### Section 409. Qualification of President and Members of Tribunal
(1) The President must be a person who is, or has been, a Judge of a High Court for five years.

(2) A person is qualified for appointment as a Judicial Member only if he:
- (a) is, or has been, a Judge of a High Court;
- (b) is, or has been, a District Judge for at least five years; or
- (c) has been an advocate of a court for at least ten years.

Explanation: In computing the period of practice as an advocate under clause (c), any period after becoming an advocate during which the person held judicial office, was a member of a tribunal, or held any post under the Union or a State requiring special knowledge of law, is included.

(3) A person is qualified for appointment as a Technical Member only if he:
- (a) has been a member of the Indian Corporate Law Service or Indian Legal Service for at least fifteen years, including at least three years in the pay scale of Joint Secretary to the Government of India, or equivalent or above, in that service;
- (b) is, or has been, a chartered accountant for at least fifteen years;
- (c) is, or has been, a cost accountant for at least fifteen years;
- (d) is, or has been, a company secretary for at least fifteen years;
- (e) is a person of proven ability, integrity and standing, with special knowledge and experience of at least fifteen years in law, industrial finance, industrial management or administration, industrial reconstruction, investment, accountancy, labour matters, or other disciplines related to management, conduct of affairs, revival, rehabilitation and winding up of companies; or
- (f) is, or has been, the presiding officer of a Labour Court, Tribunal or National Tribunal under the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947 for at least five years.

(As amended by the Companies (Amendment) Act, 2017 [verify]. Qualifications, appointment, term of office, salaries and allowances, resignation, removal and other conditions of service of Members appointed after 26-05-2017 are governed by section 184 of the Finance Act, 2017 and, from 2021, by the Tribunals Reforms Act, 2021 [verify].)

### Section 410. Constitution of Appellate Tribunal
The Central Government must, by notification, constitute an Appellate Tribunal called the National Company Law Appellate Tribunal, with effect from the date specified in the notification. It consists of:
- a Chairperson; and
- such number of Judicial and Technical Members, not exceeding eleven, as the Central Government considers fit, appointed by notification.

Its functions are [verify wording]:
- to hear appeals against orders of the Tribunal and of the National Financial Reporting Authority under this Act;
- to hear appeals under the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 2016 (under sections 61, 202 and 211 of that Code); and
- to hear appeals against directions, decisions or orders of the Competition Commission of India under the Competition Act, 2002 (as amended by the Finance Act, 2017).

### Section 411. Qualifications of Chairperson and Members of Appellate Tribunal
(1) The Chairperson must be a person who is, or has been, a Judge of the Supreme Court or the Chief Justice of a High Court.

(2) A Judicial Member must be a person who is, or has been, a Judge of a High Court, or who has been a Judicial Member of the Tribunal for five years.

(3) A Technical Member must be a person of proven ability, integrity and standing, with special knowledge and experience of at least twenty-five years in law, industrial finance, industrial management or administration, industrial reconstruction, investment, accountancy, labour matters, or other disciplines related to management, conduct of affairs, revival, rehabilitation and winding up of companies.

(Subject to section 184 of the Finance Act, 2017 and the Tribunals Reforms Act, 2021 for later appointees [verify].)

### Section 412. Selection of Members of Tribunal and Appellate Tribunal
(1) The President of the Tribunal, and the Chairperson and Judicial Members of the Appellate Tribunal, are appointed after consultation with the Chief Justice of India.

(2) The Members of the Tribunal and the Technical Members of the Appellate Tribunal are appointed on the recommendation of a Selection Committee consisting of [verify composition as amended in 2017]:
- (a) the Chief Justice of India or his nominee — Chairperson;
- (b) a senior Judge of the Supreme Court or a Chief Justice of a High Court — Member;
- (c) the Secretary in the Ministry of Corporate Affairs — Member;
- (d) the Secretary in the Ministry of Law and Justice — Member; and
- (e) the Secretary in the Department of Financial Services, Ministry of Finance — Member.

(3) The Secretary, Ministry of Corporate Affairs, is the Convener of the Selection Committee.

(4) The Selection Committee decides its own procedure for recommending persons under sub-section (2).

(5) No appointment of Members of the Tribunal or Appellate Tribunal is invalid merely because of a vacancy or defect in the constitution of the Selection Committee.

(For appointments after the Tribunals Reforms Act, 2021, selection is by a Search-cum-Selection Committee under section 3 of that Act [verify].)

### Section 413. Term of office of President, Chairperson and other Members
(1) The President and every other Member of the Tribunal hold office for five years from the date they enter upon office, and are eligible for re-appointment for another term of five years.

(2) A Member of the Tribunal holds office until he reaches:
- (a) sixty-seven years of age, in the case of the President; and
- (b) sixty-five years of age, in the case of any other Member.

Provisos:
- A person who has not completed fifty years of age is not eligible for appointment as a Member.
- A Member may retain his lien with his parent cadre, Ministry or Department while holding office, for a period not exceeding one year.

(3) The Chairperson and Members of the Appellate Tribunal hold office for five years from the date they enter upon office, and are eligible for re-appointment for another term of five years.

(4) A Member of the Appellate Tribunal holds office until he reaches:
- (a) seventy years of age, in the case of the Chairperson; and
- (b) sixty-seven years of age, in the case of any other Member.

The same provisos as in sub-section (2) apply: a minimum age of fifty years for appointment, and retention of lien for up to one year.

(Under the Tribunals Reforms Act, 2021, the term for later appointees is four years, or until the age of seventy years for a Chairperson [verify: President] and sixty-seven years for other Members, whichever is earlier. The minimum age is fifty years [verify].)

### Section 414. Salary, allowances and other terms and conditions of service of Members
The salary, allowances and other terms and conditions of service of the Members of the Tribunal and Appellate Tribunal are as prescribed. None of these may be varied to a Member's disadvantage after appointment. (Subject to the Finance Act, 2017 and the Tribunals Reforms Act, 2021 [verify].)

### Section 415. Acting President and Chairperson of Tribunal or Appellate Tribunal
(1) If the office of President or Chairperson falls vacant because of death, resignation or otherwise, the senior-most Member acts as President or Chairperson until a new appointee enters upon office.

(2) If the President or Chairperson is unable to discharge his functions because of absence, illness or any other cause, the senior-most Member [verify: or a Member nominated by the Central Government] discharges those functions until the President or Chairperson resumes office.

### Section 416. Resignation of Members
The President, Chairperson or any Member may resign by written notice under his hand addressed to the Central Government. He continues in office until the earliest of:
- three months from the date the Central Government receives the notice;
- the date a duly appointed successor enters upon office; or
- the expiry of his term.

### Section 417. Removal of Members
(1) After consultation with the Chief Justice of India, the Central Government may remove the President, Chairperson or any Member who:
- (a) has been adjudged insolvent;
- (b) has been convicted of an offence that, in the Central Government's opinion, involves moral turpitude;
- (c) has become physically or mentally incapable of acting as such;
- (d) has acquired a financial or other interest likely to affect his functions prejudicially; or
- (e) has so abused his position that his continuance in office would harm the public interest.

A person may not be removed on the grounds in clause (d) or (e) without a reasonable opportunity of being heard.

(2) Without prejudice to sub-section (1), the President, Chairperson or a Member may be removed only by an order of the Central Government on the ground of proved misbehaviour or incapacity. The order must follow an inquiry by a Judge of the Supreme Court, nominated by the Chief Justice of India on a reference from the Central Government. In that inquiry, the person must have been informed of the charges and given a reasonable opportunity of being heard.

(3) With the concurrence of the Chief Justice of India, the Central Government may suspend the President, Chairperson or a Member against whom a reference has been made under sub-section (2). The suspension lasts until the Central Government passes orders on the Judge's report.

(4) After consultation with the Supreme Court, the Central Government must make rules regulating the procedure for the inquiry under sub-section (2).

### Section 418. Staff of Tribunal and Appellate Tribunal
(1) In consultation with the Tribunal and Appellate Tribunal, the Central Government must provide them with the officers and other employees needed for the exercise of their powers and discharge of their functions.

(2) These officers and employees work under the general superintendence and control of the President or Chairperson, as the case may be, or of any Member to whom that power is delegated.

(3) Their salaries, allowances and other conditions of service are as prescribed.

### Section 418A. Benches of Appellate Tribunal
(Inserted by the Companies (Amendment) Act, 2017 [verify].)

(1) The powers of the Appellate Tribunal may be exercised by Benches, constituted by the Chairperson out of its Members.

(2) A Bench consists of at least one Judicial Member and one Technical Member [verify].

(3) The Benches of the Appellate Tribunal ordinarily sit at New Delhi, or at such other places as the Central Government, in consultation with the Chairperson, may notify. (A Bench at Chennai has been notified [verify].)

### Section 419. Benches of Tribunal
(1) The Tribunal has such number of Benches as the Central Government may specify by notification.

(2) The Principal Bench of the Tribunal is at New Delhi and is presided over by the President.

(3) The Tribunal's powers are exercised by Benches of two Members: one Judicial Member and one Technical Member. Provisos:
- Members authorised for the purpose may function as a single-member Bench of a Judicial Member, and exercise the Tribunal's powers for such classes of cases, or matters in such cases, as the President specifies by general or special order.
- If, at any stage of hearing such a case or matter, the Member considers that it ought to be heard by a two-Member Bench, the President may transfer it (or it may be referred to him for transfer) to such Bench as he considers fit.

(4) For disposal of any case relating to rehabilitation, restructuring or winding up of companies, the President must constitute one or more Special Benches of three or more Members, the majority of whom must be Judicial Members.

(5) If the Members of a Bench differ in opinion on any point:
- the point is decided by the majority, if there is one;
- if the Members are equally divided, they state the points of difference, and the President refers the case for hearing on those points by one or more other Members. The points are then decided by the majority of all the Members who heard the case, including those who first heard it.

### Section 420. Orders of Tribunal
(1) After giving the parties to a proceeding a reasonable opportunity of being heard, the Tribunal may pass such orders as it thinks fit.

(2) Within two years from the date of an order, the Tribunal may amend it to rectify a mistake apparent from the record. It must do so if a party brings the mistake to its notice. No amendment may be made to an order against which an appeal has been filed under this Act.

(3) The Tribunal must send a copy of every order passed under this section to all parties concerned.

### Section 421. Appeal from orders of Tribunal
(1) Any person aggrieved by an order of the Tribunal may appeal to the Appellate Tribunal.

(2) No appeal lies against an order made by the Tribunal with the consent of the parties.

(3) An appeal must be filed within forty-five days from the date a copy of the Tribunal's order is made available to the aggrieved person. It must be in the prescribed form and accompanied by the prescribed fee. The Appellate Tribunal may entertain an appeal filed within a further period of up to forty-five days, if satisfied that the appellant was prevented by sufficient cause from filing in time.

(4) After giving the parties a reasonable opportunity of being heard, the Appellate Tribunal may pass such orders as it thinks fit, confirming, modifying or setting aside the order appealed against.

(5) The Appellate Tribunal must send a copy of every order it makes to the Tribunal and to the parties to the appeal.

### Section 422. Expeditious disposal by Tribunal and Appellate Tribunal
(1) The Tribunal and the Appellate Tribunal must deal with and dispose of every application, petition and appeal as quickly as possible. They must make every effort to dispose of it within three months from the date it was presented or filed.

(2) If an application, petition or appeal is not disposed of within that period, the Tribunal or Appellate Tribunal must record its reasons. After considering those reasons, the President or Chairperson may extend the period by up to ninety days, as he considers necessary.

### Section 423. Appeal to Supreme Court
Any person aggrieved by an order of the Appellate Tribunal may appeal to the Supreme Court, on any question of law arising out of the order, within sixty days from the date he receives the order. The Supreme Court may allow an appeal to be filed within a further period of up to sixty days, if satisfied that the appellant was prevented by sufficient cause from filing in time.

### Section 424. Procedure before Tribunal and Appellate Tribunal
(1) In disposing of any proceeding or appeal, the Tribunal and Appellate Tribunal:
- are not bound by the procedure in the Code of Civil Procedure, 1908;
- are guided by the principles of natural justice; and
- subject to this Act, the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 2016 and the rules, may regulate their own procedure.

(2) For discharging their functions under this Act or the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 2016, the Tribunal and Appellate Tribunal have the same powers as a civil court trying a suit under the Code of Civil Procedure, 1908, in respect of:
- (a) summoning and enforcing the attendance of any person and examining him on oath;
- (b) requiring discovery and production of documents;
- (c) receiving evidence on affidavits;
- (d) subject to sections 123 and 124 of the Indian Evidence Act, 1872, requisitioning any public record or document, or a copy of it, from any office;
- (e) issuing commissions for examining witnesses or documents;
- (f) dismissing a representation for default, or deciding it ex parte;
- (g) setting aside any order dismissing a representation for default, or any order passed ex parte; and
- (h) any other prescribed matter.

(3) An order of the Tribunal or Appellate Tribunal may be enforced by that Tribunal as if it were a decree of a court in a suit pending before it. The Tribunal may send its orders for execution to the court within whose local limits:
- (a) for an order against a company, the company's registered office is situated; or
- (b) for an order against any other person, that person voluntarily resides, carries on business or personally works for gain.

(4) All proceedings before the Tribunal or Appellate Tribunal are treated as judicial proceedings within the meaning of sections 193 and 228, and for the purposes of section 196, of the Indian Penal Code. The Tribunal and Appellate Tribunal are treated as civil courts for the purposes of section 195 and Chapter XXVI of the Code of Criminal Procedure, 1973. (These references are now to be read as references to the corresponding provisions of the Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, 2023 and the Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita, 2023 [verify].)

### Section 425. Power to punish for contempt
The Tribunal and Appellate Tribunal have the same jurisdiction, powers and authority over contempt of themselves as a High Court has. For this purpose, they may exercise the powers under the Contempt of Courts Act, 1971, with these modifications:
- (a) references in that Act to a High Court include the Tribunal and the Appellate Tribunal; and
- (b) references to the Advocate-General in section 15 of that Act are to such Law Officers as the Central Government specifies.

### Section 426. Delegation of powers
By general or special order, and subject to any conditions in the order, the Tribunal or Appellate Tribunal may direct any of its officers or employees, or any other authorised person, to inquire into any matter connected with a proceeding or appeal before it, and to report to it as the order specifies.

### Section 427. President, Members, officers, etc., to be public servants
The following are treated as public servants within the meaning of section 21 of the Indian Penal Code:
- the President, Members, officers and other employees of the Tribunal; and
- the Chairperson, Members, officers and other employees of the Appellate Tribunal.

### Section 428. Protection of action taken in good faith
No suit, prosecution or other legal proceeding lies, for any loss or damage caused or likely to be caused by any act done or intended to be done in good faith under this Act, against:
- the Tribunal, its President, Members, officers or other employees;
- the Appellate Tribunal, its Chairperson, Members, officers or other employees; or
- any liquidator or other person authorised by the Tribunal or Appellate Tribunal to discharge any function under this Act.

### Section 429. Power to seek assistance of Chief Metropolitan Magistrate, etc.
(1) In any proceeding for winding up a company under this Act, or in any other proceeding [verify: as amended by the IBC, 2016], the Tribunal may request in writing the following officer to take possession of the company's property, books of account or other documents, in order to take them into its custody or control:
- the Chief Metropolitan Magistrate;
- the Chief Judicial Magistrate; or
- the District Collector,

within whose jurisdiction the property, books or documents are situated or found. On such a request, the officer must:
- (a) take possession of the property, books of account or other documents; and
- (b) have them entrusted to the Tribunal or to other persons it authorises.

(2) To secure compliance with sub-section (1), the officer may take, or cause to be taken, such steps and use, or cause to be used, such force as he considers necessary.

(3) No act of the officer done under this section may be questioned in any court or before any authority on any ground.

### Section 430. Civil court not to have jurisdiction
No civil court has jurisdiction to entertain any suit or proceeding on any matter that the Tribunal or Appellate Tribunal is empowered to decide under this Act or any other law. No court or other authority may grant an injunction against any action taken or to be taken by the Tribunal or Appellate Tribunal under any power conferred by or under this Act or any other law.

### Section 431. Vacancy in Tribunal or Appellate Tribunal not to invalidate acts or proceedings
No act or proceeding of the Tribunal or Appellate Tribunal may be questioned, or is invalid, merely because of a vacancy or defect in its constitution.

### Section 432. Right to legal representation
A party to any proceeding or appeal before the Tribunal or Appellate Tribunal may appear in person, or authorise one or more of the following to present his case:
- chartered accountants;
- company secretaries;
- cost accountants;
- legal practitioners; or
- any other person.

### Section 433. Limitation
The Limitation Act, 1963 applies, as far as may be, to proceedings and appeals before the Tribunal or Appellate Tribunal.

### Section 434. Transfer of certain pending proceedings
(1) On a date notified by the Central Government:
- (a) all matters, proceedings or cases pending before the Board of Company Law Administration (Company Law Board), constituted under section 10E(1) of the Companies Act, 1956, immediately before that date stand transferred to the Tribunal. The Tribunal disposes of them in accordance with this Act;
- (b) any person aggrieved by a decision or order of the Company Law Board made before that date may appeal to the High Court on any question of law arising out of it. The appeal must be filed within sixty days from the date the decision or order is communicated to him. The High Court may allow a further period of up to sixty days if satisfied that the appellant was prevented by sufficient cause from filing in time;
- (c) all proceedings under the Companies Act, 1956 pending immediately before that date before any District Court or High Court stand transferred to the Tribunal, which may deal with them from the stage they had reached before transfer. This includes proceedings on arbitration, compromise, arrangements, reconstruction and winding up of companies. Provisos (as substituted by the IBC, 2016 and amended by the IBC (Second Amendment) Act, 2018 [verify]):
  - only winding up proceedings that are at a stage prescribed by the Central Government are transferred to the Tribunal;
  - for cases other than winding up, only proceedings in which orders on allowing or otherwise have not been reserved by the High Court are transferred;
  - the following continue to be dealt with under the Companies Act, 1956 and the Companies (Court) Rules, 1959:
    - (i) proceedings under the Companies Act, 1956, other than winding up cases, that have been reserved for orders; and
    - (ii) winding up proceedings that have not been transferred from the High Courts;
  - voluntary winding up cases in which notice of the resolution has been advertised under section 485(1) of the Companies Act, 1956, but the company was not dissolved before 1-4-2017, continue under the Companies Act, 1956 and the Companies (Court) Rules, 1959;
  - any party to winding up proceedings pending before any court immediately before the commencement of the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code (Second Amendment) Act, 2018 may apply for transfer of the proceedings. The court may order the transfer to the Tribunal, which then deals with them as an application for initiation of corporate insolvency resolution process under the IBC, 2016; and
- (d) any appeal, reference or inquiry pending before the Board for Industrial and Financial Reconstruction or the Appellate Authority for Industrial and Financial Reconstruction under the Sick Industrial Companies (Special Provisions) Act, 1985 abates [verify placement]. A company whose appeal, reference or inquiry abates may make a reference to the Tribunal under the IBC, 2016 within one hundred and eighty days from the commencement of that Code. No fee is payable for such a reference [verify].

(2) The Central Government may make rules, consistent with this Act, to ensure the timely transfer to the Tribunal under this section of all matters, proceedings or cases pending before the Company Law Board or the courts.

## CHAPTER XXVIII — SPECIAL COURTS

### Section 435. Establishment of Special Courts
(As substituted by the Companies (Amendment) Act, 2017, effective 2018 [verify].)

(1) To provide speedy trial of offences under this Act, the Central Government may, by notification, establish or designate as many Special Courts as necessary. All offences under this Act punishable with imprisonment of two years or more are triable by a Special Court established under this sub-section. All other offences are triable by a Metropolitan Magistrate or a Judicial Magistrate of the First Class having jurisdiction [verify proviso wording].

(2) A Special Court consists of:
- (a) a single judge holding office as Sessions Judge or Additional Sessions Judge, for offences punishable under this Act with imprisonment of two years or more; and
- (b) a Metropolitan Magistrate or a Judicial Magistrate of the First Class, for other offences.

The judge or magistrate is appointed by the Central Government with the concurrence of the Chief Justice of the High Court within whose jurisdiction he is working.

(Original 2013 text: Special Courts were for offences other than under section 452. They consisted of a single judge who, immediately before appointment, held office as a Sessions Judge or Additional Sessions Judge [verify].)

### Section 436. Offences triable by Special Courts
(1) Notwithstanding the Code of Criminal Procedure, 1973:
- (a) all offences specified under section 435(1) are triable only by the Special Court established for the area in which the registered office of the company concerned is situated. If there is more than one Special Court for the area, the case goes to the one specified by the High Court concerned;
- (b) where a person accused or suspected of an offence under this Act is forwarded to a Magistrate under section 167(2) or (2A) of that Code, the Magistrate may authorise his detention in such custody as he thinks fit, for up to:
  - fifteen days in total, where the Magistrate is a Judicial Magistrate; and
  - seven days in total, where the Magistrate is an Executive Magistrate.

  If the Magistrate considers that detention is unnecessary, on or before the end of the detention period, he must order the person to be forwarded to the Special Court having jurisdiction;
- (c) the Special Court may exercise, in relation to a person forwarded to it under clause (b), the same power that a Magistrate having jurisdiction to try the case may exercise under section 167 of that Code; and
- (d) the Special Court may take cognizance of an offence without the accused being committed to it for trial, on reading a police report of the facts constituting an offence under this Act, or on a complaint.

(2) When trying an offence under this Act, a Special Court may also try any other offence with which the accused may be charged at the same trial under the Code of Criminal Procedure, 1973.

(3) Notwithstanding that Code, the Special Court may, if it thinks fit, try summarily any offence under this Act punishable with imprisonment of up to three years. Provisos:
- On conviction in a summary trial, no sentence of imprisonment exceeding one year may be passed.
- If, at the start or during a summary trial, the Special Court finds that a sentence exceeding one year may be needed, or that a summary trial is otherwise undesirable, it must record an order to that effect after hearing the parties. It then recalls any witnesses already examined and hears or rehears the case under the procedure for a regular trial.

(References to the Code of Criminal Procedure, 1973 are now to be read as references to the corresponding provisions of the Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita, 2023 [verify].)

### Section 437. Appeal and revision
The High Court may exercise, so far as applicable, all the powers conferred on a High Court by Chapters XXIX and XXX of the Code of Criminal Procedure, 1973, as if a Special Court within its local jurisdiction were a Court of Session trying cases within that jurisdiction.

### Section 438. Application of Code to proceedings before Special Court
Except as otherwise provided in this Act, the Code of Criminal Procedure, 1973 applies to proceedings before a Special Court. For the purposes of that Code:
- the Special Court is treated as a Court of Session, or as the court of a Metropolitan Magistrate or Judicial Magistrate of the First Class, as the case may be [verify: 2017 amendment]; and
- the person conducting the prosecution is treated as a Public Prosecutor.

### Section 439. Offences to be non-cognizable
(1) Notwithstanding the Code of Criminal Procedure, 1973, every offence under this Act is treated as non-cognizable within the meaning of that Code, except the offences referred to in section 212(6).

(2) No court may take cognizance of an offence under this Act alleged to have been committed by a company or any of its officers, except on a written complaint by:
- the Registrar;
- a shareholder of the company; or
- a person authorised by the Central Government.

Provisos:
- A court may take cognizance of offences relating to issue and transfer of securities and non-payment of dividend on a written complaint by a person authorised by the Securities and Exchange Board of India.
- This sub-section does not apply to a prosecution by a company of any of its officers.

(3) Notwithstanding that Code, where the complainant under sub-section (2) is the Registrar or a person authorised by the Central Government, that officer need not appear before the court trying the offence, unless the court requires his personal attendance at the trial.

(4) Sub-section (2) does not apply to any action taken by the liquidator of a company for an offence alleged to have been committed in respect of any matter in Chapter XX, or any other provision of this Act relating to winding up.

Explanation: For this section, the liquidator of a company is not treated as an officer of the company.

### Section 440. Transitional provisions
Until a Special Court is established, any offence under this Act that is triable by a Special Court is tried by the Court of Session exercising jurisdiction over the area, or by the Metropolitan Magistrate or Judicial Magistrate of the First Class, as the case may be [verify: 2017 amendment]. This applies notwithstanding the Code of Criminal Procedure, 1973. This section does not affect the High Court's power under section 407 of that Code to transfer any case or class of cases taken cognizance of under this section.

### Section 441. Compounding of certain offences
(As amended by the Companies (Amendment) Acts of 2019 and 2020 [verify].)

(1) Notwithstanding the Code of Criminal Procedure, 1973, an offence under this Act that is punishable with fine only, or with fine or imprisonment or both [verify: 2020 wording], may be compounded before or after a prosecution is instituted. This applies whether the offence was committed by a company or any of its officers. The offence may be compounded by:
- (a) the Tribunal; or
- (b) where the maximum fine for the offence does not exceed twenty-five lakh rupees (originally five lakh rupees; raised by the Companies (Amendment) Act, 2020 [verify]), the Regional Director or any officer authorised by the Central Government.

Compounding is on payment or credit to the Central Government, by the company or officer, of the sum specified by the Tribunal, Regional Director or authorised officer. Provisos:
- The sum specified may not exceed the maximum fine that may be imposed for the offence compounded.
- In specifying the sum, any amount paid as additional fee under section 403(2) is taken into account.
- An offence by a company or its officer may not be compounded under this sub-section if an investigation against the company has been initiated or is pending under this Act.

(2) Sub-section (1) does not apply to an offence committed by a company or its officer within three years from the date on which a similar offence committed by it or him was compounded under this section.

Explanation: For this section:
- (a) a second or subsequent offence committed after three years from the date the earlier offence was compounded is treated as a first offence; and
- (b) "Regional Director" means a person appointed by the Central Government as a Regional Director for the purposes of this Act.

(3) Procedure:
- (a) Every application for compounding is made to the Registrar, who forwards it with his comments to the Tribunal, Regional Director or authorised officer, as the case may be.
- (b) Where an offence is compounded, before or after a prosecution is instituted, the company must inform the Registrar within seven days from the date of compounding.
- (c) Where an offence is compounded before a prosecution is instituted, no prosecution may be instituted for it against the offender, whether by the Registrar, a shareholder, or a person authorised by the Central Government.
- (d) Where an offence is compounded after a prosecution is instituted, the Registrar must bring the composition to the notice of the court in writing. On such notice, the company or officer concerned is discharged.

(4) This sub-section applies when the Tribunal, Regional Director or authorised officer deals with a proposal to compound a default in complying with a provision that requires a company or its officer to file or register with, or deliver or send to, the Registrar any return, account or other document. It may, by order, direct any officer or other employee of the company to file or register the return, account or document within the time specified in the order, on payment of the fee and additional fee under section 403.

(5) An officer or employee who fails to comply with an order under sub-section (4) is punishable with imprisonment up to six months, or a fine up to one lakh rupees, or both.

(6) Notwithstanding the Code of Criminal Procedure, 1973:
- (a) [verify: omitted by the Companies (Amendment) Act, 2020. The original text made offences punishable with imprisonment or fine, or both, compoundable with the permission of the Special Court]; and
- (b) an offence punishable under this Act with imprisonment only, or with imprisonment and also fine, is not compoundable.

(7) No offence under this section may be compounded except under and in accordance with this section.

### Section 442. Mediation and Conciliation Panel
(1) The Central Government must maintain a panel of experts, called the Mediation and Conciliation Panel, with the prescribed number of experts and prescribed qualifications. The panel mediates between parties during proceedings under this Act before the Central Government, the Tribunal or the Appellate Tribunal.

(2) At any time during such proceedings, any party may apply to the Central Government, Tribunal or Appellate Tribunal to refer the matter to the Mediation and Conciliation Panel. The application must be in the prescribed form, with the prescribed fee. The authority concerned must then appoint one or more experts from the panel.

(3) The Central Government, Tribunal or Appellate Tribunal before which a proceeding is pending may also, on its own motion, refer any matter in the proceeding to such number of experts from the panel as it considers fit.

(4) The fee and other terms and conditions of the experts are as prescribed.

(5) The Mediation and Conciliation Panel must follow the prescribed procedure and dispose of the matter within three months from the date of reference. It then forwards its recommendations to the Central Government, Tribunal or Appellate Tribunal.

(6) Any party aggrieved by the panel's recommendation may file objections with the Central Government, Tribunal or Appellate Tribunal, as the case may be.

### Section 443. Power of Central Government to appoint company prosecutors
Subject to section 24 of the Code of Criminal Procedure, 1973, the Central Government may appoint one or more persons as company prosecutors to conduct prosecutions arising out of this Act. Appointments may be general, for any case, or for any specified class of cases in any local area. Company prosecutors have all the powers and privileges conferred by that Code on Public Prosecutors appointed under section 24.

### Section 444. Appeal against acquittal
Notwithstanding the Code of Criminal Procedure, 1973, in any case arising under this Act, the Central Government may direct a company prosecutor, or authorise any other person by name or office, to file an appeal against an order of acquittal passed by any court other than a High Court. An appeal so filed is treated as validly presented to the appellate court.

### Section 445. Compensation for accusation without reasonable cause
Section 250 of the Code of Criminal Procedure, 1973 applies to cases instituted under this Act on a complaint or information. That section provides for compensation to an accused where an accusation was made without reasonable cause [verify wording].

### Section 446. Application of fines
A court imposing a fine under this Act may direct that the whole or any part of it be applied towards:
- the costs of the proceedings; or
- a reward to the person on whose information the proceedings were instituted.

### Section 446A. Factors for determining level of punishment
(Inserted by the Companies (Amendment) Act, 2017 [verify].) When deciding the amount of fine or imprisonment under this Act, the court or Special Court must have due regard to the following factors:
- (a) the size of the company;
- (b) the nature of the business carried on by the company;
- (c) injury to public interest;
- (d) the nature of the default; and
- (e) repetition of the default.

### Section 446B. Lesser penalties for certain companies
(Inserted by the Companies (Amendment) Act, 2017; substituted or amended by the Companies (Amendment) Act, 2020 [verify].) Notwithstanding anything in this Act, this section applies where a penalty is payable for non-compliance with any provision of this Act by:
- a One Person Company;
- a small company;
- a start-up company; or
- a Producer Company,

or by any officer in default or other person in respect of such a company. The penalty payable is not more than one-half of the penalty specified in the provision concerned, subject to a maximum of:
- two lakh rupees, for the company; and
- one lakh rupees, for an officer in default or other person.

Explanation: For this section, "start-up company" means a private company incorporated under this Act or under any previous company law that fulfils the conditions notified by the Central Government from time to time under section 2(72) of this Act or otherwise [verify].

## CHAPTER XXIX — MISCELLANEOUS

### Section 447. Punishment for fraud
Without prejudice to any liability under this Act or any other law, including repayment of any debt, this section applies to a person found guilty of fraud involving an amount of at least ten lakh rupees, or one per cent of the company's turnover, whichever is lower. Such a person is punishable with:
- imprisonment of not less than six months, which may extend to ten years; and
- a fine of not less than the amount involved in the fraud, which may extend to three times that amount.

Provisos:
- Where the fraud involves public interest, the term of imprisonment is not less than three years.
- Where the fraud involves an amount less than ten lakh rupees or one per cent of the company's turnover, whichever is lower, and does not involve public interest, the person is punishable with imprisonment up to five years, or a fine up to fifty lakh rupees, or both. (This proviso was inserted by the Companies (Amendment) Act, 2017 with a fine of twenty lakh rupees, raised to fifty lakh rupees by the Companies (Amendment) Act, 2019 [verify].)

Explanation: For this section:
- (i) "fraud", in relation to the affairs of a company or body corporate, includes any act, omission, concealment of any fact, or abuse of position, committed by any person (or by any other person with his connivance) in any manner, with intent to:
  - deceive;
  - gain undue advantage from the company, its shareholders, its creditors or any other person; or
  - injure their interests,

  whether or not there is any wrongful gain or wrongful loss;
- (ii) "wrongful gain" means the gain by unlawful means of property to which the person gaining is not legally entitled; and
- (iii) "wrongful loss" means the loss by unlawful means of property to which the person losing is legally entitled.

### Section 448. Punishment for false statement
Except as otherwise provided in this Act, a person is liable under section 447 if, in any return, report, certificate, financial statement, prospectus, statement or other document required by or for the purposes of this Act or the rules, he makes a statement:
- (a) that is false in any material particular, knowing it to be false; or
- (b) that omits any material fact, knowing it to be material.

### Section 449. Punishment for false evidence
Except as otherwise provided in this Act, a person who intentionally gives false evidence is punishable with imprisonment of not less than three years, which may extend to seven years, and a fine which may extend to ten lakh rupees. This applies to false evidence given:
- (a) on any examination on oath or solemn affirmation authorised under this Act; or
- (b) in any affidavit, deposition or solemn affirmation in or about the winding up of any company under this Act, or otherwise in or about any matter arising under this Act.

### Section 450. Punishment where no specific penalty or punishment is provided
(As substituted by the Companies (Amendment) Act, 2019 [verify].) This section applies where a company, an officer of a company or any other person contravenes:
- any provision of this Act or the rules; or
- any condition, limitation or restriction subject to which any approval, sanction, consent, confirmation, recognition, direction or exemption has been granted,

and no penalty or punishment for the contravention is provided elsewhere in this Act. The company, every officer in default, or the other person is liable to:
- a penalty of ten thousand rupees; and
- for a continuing contravention, a further penalty of one thousand rupees for each day after the first that it continues,

subject to a maximum of two lakh rupees for a company, and fifty thousand rupees for an officer in default or any other person.

(Original 2013 text: a fine up to ten thousand rupees, with a further fine up to one thousand rupees per day for a continuing contravention [verify].)

### Section 451. Punishment in case of repeated default
If a company or an officer of a company commits an offence punishable with fine or imprisonment, and commits the same offence again on a second or subsequent occasion within three years, the company and every officer in default are punishable with twice the amount of fine for the offence. This is in addition to any imprisonment provided for that offence. [verify: whether a proviso was added excluding defaults adjudicated under section 454 or offences already compounded.]

### Section 452. Punishment for wrongful withholding of property
(1) An officer or employee of a company is punishable with a fine of not less than one lakh rupees, which may extend to five lakh rupees, if he:
- (a) wrongfully obtains possession of any property of the company, including cash; or
- (b) having any such property (including cash) in his possession, wrongfully withholds it or knowingly applies it for purposes other than those expressed or directed in the articles and authorised by this Act.

He is liable on the complaint of the company or any member, creditor or contributory.

(2) The court trying the offence may also order the officer or employee to deliver up or refund, within a time it fixes, any property or cash wrongfully obtained, wrongfully withheld or knowingly misapplied, and the receipts of any undue benefits derived from it. In default, he is liable to imprisonment up to two years.

### Section 453. Punishment for improper use of "Limited" or "Private Limited"
This section applies where any person carries on business under a name or title whose last word or words are "Limited" or "Private Limited", or any contraction or imitation of them, without being duly incorporated with limited liability, or as a private company with limited liability, as the case may be. That person, or each such person, is liable to a penalty of five hundred rupees for each day the name or title has been used, subject to a maximum of two lakh rupees [verify: as substituted by the Companies (Amendment) Act, 2020].

(Original 2013 text: a fine of not less than five hundred rupees, which may extend to two thousand rupees, for every day the name or title was used [verify].)

### Section 454. Adjudication of penalties
(1) By an order published in the Official Gazette, the Central Government may appoint as many of its officers, not below the rank of Registrar, as adjudicating officers to adjudge penalties under this Act in the prescribed manner.

(2) When appointing adjudicating officers, the Central Government must specify their jurisdiction in the order under sub-section (1).

(3) The adjudicating officer may, by order:
- (a) impose a penalty on the company, the officer in default or any other person, stating the non-compliance or default under the relevant provisions of this Act; and
- (b) direct the company, officer in default or other person to rectify the default, wherever he considers fit.

(As substituted or amended by the Companies (Amendment) Act, 2019 [verify].)

Proviso: No penalty is imposed, and all proceedings under this section for the default are treated as concluded, if both of the following apply:
- the default relates to non-compliance with section 92(4) (filing of annual return) or section 137(1) or (2) (filing of financial statements); and
- the default has been rectified either before, or within thirty days of, the issue of notice by the adjudicating officer [verify].

(4) Before imposing any penalty, the adjudicating officer must give the company, officer in default or other person a reasonable opportunity of being heard.

(5) Any person aggrieved by an order under sub-section (3) may appeal to the Regional Director having jurisdiction.

(6) An appeal under sub-section (5) must be filed within sixty days from the date the aggrieved person receives a copy of the adjudicating officer's order. It must be in the prescribed form and manner and accompanied by the prescribed fee.

(7) After giving the parties an opportunity of being heard, the Regional Director may pass such order as he thinks fit, confirming, modifying or setting aside the order appealed against.

(8) Consequences of non-payment of a penalty imposed by the adjudicating officer or Regional Director within ninety days from the date of receipt of the order:
- (i) the company is punishable with a fine of not less than twenty-five thousand rupees, which may extend to five lakh rupees; and
- (ii) an officer in default or other person is punishable with imprisonment up to six months, or a fine of not less than twenty-five thousand rupees which may extend to one lakh rupees, or both [verify].

(Penalty proceedings are conducted under the Companies (Adjudication of Penalties) Rules, 2014, as amended, including e-adjudication through the MCA portal [verify].)

### Section 454A. Penalty for repeated default
(Inserted by the Companies (Amendment) Act, 2020 [verify].) This section applies where a company, an officer of a company or any other person has already been penalised for a default under this Act, and commits the same default again within three years from the date of the order imposing the penalty, passed by the adjudicating officer or the Regional Director. For the second or subsequent default, it or he is liable to an amount equal to twice the penalty provided for that default under the relevant provision of this Act.

### Section 455. Dormant company
(1) A company may apply to the Registrar, in the prescribed manner, for the status of a dormant company if it is either:
- a company formed and registered under this Act for a future project, or to hold an asset or intellectual property, that has no significant accounting transaction; or
- an inactive company.

Explanation: For this section:
- (i) "inactive company" means a company that, during the last two financial years:
  - has not been carrying on any business or operation;
  - has not made any significant accounting transaction; or
  - has not filed financial statements and annual returns;
- (ii) "significant accounting transaction" means any transaction other than:
  - (a) payment of fees by the company to the Registrar;
  - (b) payments made by it to fulfil the requirements of this Act or any other law;
  - (c) allotment of shares to fulfil the requirements of this Act; and
  - (d) payments for maintenance of its office and records.

(2) On considering the application, the Registrar must grant the applicant the status of a dormant company and issue a certificate in the prescribed form.

(3) The Registrar must maintain a register of dormant companies in the prescribed form.

(4) If a company has not filed financial statements or annual returns for two consecutive financial years, the Registrar must issue a notice to it and enter its name in the register of dormant companies.

(5) To retain its dormant status, a dormant company must:
- have the prescribed minimum number of directors;
- file the prescribed documents; and
- pay the prescribed annual fee to the Registrar.

It may become an active company on an application made with the prescribed documents and fee.

(6) The Registrar must strike off from the register of dormant companies the name of any dormant company that fails to comply with this section.

### Section 456. Protection of action taken in good faith
No suit, prosecution or other legal proceeding lies against the Government, any officer of the Government, or any other person for:
- anything done or intended to be done in good faith under this Act or any rules or orders made under it; or
- the publication, by or under the authority of the Government or such officer, of any report, paper or proceedings.

### Section 457. Non-disclosure of information in certain cases
Notwithstanding anything in this Act, the Central Government, or any officer or authority under its control, cannot be compelled to disclose to any court, Tribunal or other authority the source from which it received information that:
- led to an investigation being ordered under this Act; or
- is relevant or material to such an investigation [verify wording and scope].

### Section 458. Delegation by Central Government of its powers and functions
(1) The Central Government may, by notification, delegate any of its powers or functions under this Act (other than the power to make rules) to any authority or officer specified in the notification. The delegation is subject to any conditions, limitations and restrictions specified in the notification [verify any proviso].

(2) A copy of every notification under sub-section (1) must be laid before each House of Parliament as soon as may be after it is issued.

### Section 459. Powers of Central Government or Tribunal to accord approval, etc., subject to conditions and to prescribe fees on applications
(1) This sub-section applies where any provision of this Act requires or authorises the Central Government or the Tribunal:
- (a) to accord approval, sanction, consent, confirmation or recognition to, or in relation to, any matter;
- (b) to give any direction in relation to any matter; or
- (c) to grant any exemption in relation to any matter.

Unless that or any other provision of this Act provides otherwise, the Central Government or Tribunal may grant the approval, sanction, consent, confirmation, recognition, direction or exemption subject to such conditions, limitations or restrictions as it thinks fit. If any of them is contravened, it may rescind or withdraw the grant.

(2) Except as otherwise expressly provided in this Act, every application to the Central Government or Tribunal under any provision of this Act must be accompanied by the prescribed fee. This applies to applications:
- (a) for any approval, sanction, consent, confirmation or recognition;
- (b) for any direction or exemption; or
- (c) in respect of any other matter.

Different fees may be prescribed for applications by different classes of companies.

### Section 460. Condonation of delay in certain cases
Notwithstanding anything in this Act:
- (a) where an application required to be made to the Central Government under any provision of this Act is not made within the time specified, the Central Government may condone the delay, for reasons recorded in writing; and
- (b) where a document required to be filed with the Registrar under any provision of this Act is not filed within the time specified, the Central Government may condone the delay, for reasons recorded in writing [verify: any proviso, for example relating to delay governed by section 403].

### Section 461. Annual report by Central Government
The Central Government must have a general annual report on the working and administration of this Act prepared and laid before each House of Parliament within one year of the close of the year to which it relates.

### Section 462. Power to exempt class or classes of companies from provisions of this Act
(1) In the public interest, the Central Government may, by notification, direct that any provision of this Act:
- (a) does not apply to such class or classes of companies as may be specified; or
- (b) applies to such class or classes of companies with the exceptions, modifications and adaptations specified in the notification.

(2) A copy of every notification proposed under sub-section (1) must be laid in draft before each House of Parliament, while it is in session, for a total of thirty days. If both Houses agree to disapprove it, the notification must not be issued. If both Houses agree to modify it, it may be issued only in the modified form agreed by both Houses.

(3) In counting the period of thirty days under sub-section (2), no account is taken of any period during which the House is prorogued or adjourned for more than four consecutive days.

(4) A copy of every notification issued under sub-section (1) must be laid before each House of Parliament.

(Exemption notifications under this section have been issued for, among others, Government companies, private companies, Section 8 companies, Nidhis [verify] and specified IFSC companies.)

### Section 463. Power of court to grant relief in certain cases
(1) In any proceeding against an officer of a company for negligence, default, breach of duty, misfeasance or breach of trust, the court hearing the case may relieve him wholly or partly from liability, on such terms as it thinks fit, if it appears that:
- he is or may be liable for the negligence, default, breach of duty, misfeasance or breach of trust; but
- he acted honestly and reasonably; and
- having regard to all the circumstances, including those connected with his appointment, he ought fairly to be excused.

In a criminal proceeding under this sub-section, the court has no power to grant relief from any civil liability that may attach to the officer for such negligence, default, breach of duty, misfeasance or breach of trust.

(2) An officer who has reason to apprehend that a proceeding will or might be brought against him for negligence, default, breach of duty, misfeasance or breach of trust may apply to the High Court for relief. The High Court has the same power to relieve him as if it were the court before which such a proceeding had been brought under sub-section (1).

(3) No court may grant relief under sub-section (1) or (2) unless it has required the Registrar, and any other person it thinks necessary, by notice served in the manner it specifies, to show cause why the relief should not be granted.

### Section 464. Prohibition of association or partnership of persons exceeding certain number
(1) No association or partnership of more than the prescribed number of persons may be formed to carry on any business whose object is gain by the association or partnership or by its individual members, unless it is registered as a company under this Act or formed under any other law. The number prescribed may not exceed one hundred. (Rule 10 of the Companies (Miscellaneous) Rules, 2014 prescribes fifty [verify].)

(2) Sub-section (1) does not apply to:
- (a) a Hindu undivided family carrying on any business; or
- (b) an association or partnership formed by professionals who are governed by special Acts.

(3) Every member of an association or partnership carrying on business in contravention of sub-section (1) is:
- punishable with a fine which may extend to one lakh rupees; and
- personally liable for all liabilities incurred in that business.

### Section 465. Repeal of certain enactments and savings
(1) The Companies Act, 1956 and the Registration of Companies (Sikkim) Act, 1961 (the "repealed enactments") are repealed.

(The proviso continuing Part IXA of the Companies Act, 1956 for Producer Companies until a special Act was enacted was omitted when Chapter XXIA was inserted by the Companies (Amendment) Act, 2020 [verify]. A further proviso dealt with continuance of provisions of the repealed Act until the corresponding provisions of this Act were notified [verify].)

(2) Notwithstanding the repeal of the repealed enactments:
- (a) anything done or any action taken (or purported to have been done or taken) under the repealed enactments is treated as done or taken under the corresponding provisions of this Act, so far as not inconsistent with this Act. This includes any rule, notification, inspection, order or notice made or issued, any appointment, confirmation or declaration made, any licence, permission, authorisation or exemption granted, any document or instrument executed, and any direction given;
- (b) subject to clause (a), any of the following made, directed, passed, given, taken, executed, issued or done under any repealed enactment continues in force if in force at the commencement of this Act, and has effect as if done under this Act: any order, rule, notification, regulation, appointment, conveyance, mortgage, deed, document or agreement, fee directed, resolution passed, direction given, proceeding taken, instrument executed or issued, or thing done;
- (c) any principle or rule of law, established jurisdiction, form or course of pleading, practice or procedure, existing usage, custom, privilege, restriction or exemption is not affected, even if it was affirmed, recognised or derived by, in or from the repealed enactments;
- (d) [verify: further clauses, covering among other things:
  - continuance of registers, indexes and documents kept under the repealed enactments;
  - continuance of offices of Registrars;
  - companies registered under the repealed enactments being treated as registered under this Act;
  - pending prosecutions and proceedings; and
  - the application of section 6 of the General Clauses Act, 1897.]

### Section 466. Dissolution of Company Law Board and consequential provisions
(1) On the constitution of the National Company Law Tribunal and Appellate Tribunal under this Act, the Board of Company Law Administration (Company Law Board) constituted under the Companies Act, 1956 is dissolved [verify any proviso on continued functioning until constitution].

(2) On the dissolution of the Company Law Board, the following vacate their offices: the Chairman, Vice-Chairman and Members of the Company Law Board, and every officer and employee holding office immediately before the dissolution. They are entitled to claim compensation, not exceeding three months' pay and allowances, for the premature termination of their term of office or of any contract of service. Provisos:
- Officers and employees appointed to the Company Law Board on deputation are, on its dissolution, repatriated to their parent cadre, Ministry or Department.
- Officers and employees of the Company Law Board employed on a regular basis become officers and employees of the Tribunal. They keep the same rights and privileges as to pension, gratuity and similar matters as they would have had if not transferred. They continue so until their employment in the Tribunal is duly terminated, or their remuneration and terms and conditions of employment are duly altered by the Tribunal.

### Section 467. Power of Central Government to amend Schedules
(1) Subject to this section, the Central Government may, by notification, alter any of the regulations, rules, tables, forms and other provisions in any Schedule to this Act.

(2) An alteration notified under sub-section (1) has effect as if enacted in this Act. It comes into force on the date of the notification, unless the notification directs otherwise.

(3) Every alteration under sub-section (1) must be laid before each House of Parliament as soon as may be after it is made [verify: any provision on modification or annulment by Parliament].

### Section 468. Powers of Central Government to make rules relating to winding up
(1) The Central Government must make rules, consistent with the Code of Civil Procedure, 1908, providing for all matters relating to winding up of companies that this Act requires to be prescribed. It may also make rules on any other matters needed to give effect to the winding up provisions of this Act.

(2) In particular, and without limiting sub-section (1), the rules may provide for all or any of the following [verify list]:
- the mode of proceedings in a winding up by the Tribunal;
- the holding of meetings of creditors and members in connection with proceedings under section 230;
- giving effect to the provisions of this Act on reduction of share capital;
- generally, all applications to be made to the Tribunal under this Act;
- the holding and conduct of meetings to ascertain the wishes of creditors and contributories;
- the settling of lists of contributories, rectification of the register of members, and collection and application of assets;
- the payment, delivery, conveyance, surrender or transfer of money, property, books or papers to the liquidator;
- the making of calls; and
- the fixing of a time within which debts and claims must be proved.

(3) Rules made under this section are laid before Parliament in the same manner as rules under section 469 [verify].

### Section 469. Power of Central Government to make rules
(1) The Central Government may, by notification, make rules to carry out the provisions of this Act.

(2) Without limiting sub-section (1), the rules may provide for any matter that this Act requires or allows to be prescribed, or to be provided for by rules.
